#5662. How The Developers Of '007 First Light' Nailed The Feeling Of Being James Bond Through Its Cars And Vehicles

As a massive fan of the James Bond franchise, my main hope going into "007 First Light" wasn't that it would have characters and plot points I recognized or have all the right bits of fan service — I just wanted the game to evoke the same emotions that the Bond movies do, to really feel like I was being Bond when playing. Luckily the game delivered those feelings in spades. IO Interactive, best known for the "Hitman" franchise, created one of the best pieces of James Bond media ever, and a big part of "First Light's" success is down to the vehicles you get to drive as Bond and how they're woven into the main story.

To learn more about the game's development, and the creation of the new DLC missions that feature the Aston Martin Valhalla, I hopped on a call with Rasmus Poulsen, the game's art director, and Dominic Vega, audio director and game director of live content/missions. "We knew from the get-go that we wanted as wide a range of driving experiences as we could manage to squeeze in the game, and of course that means everything from the unexpected to the hoped for," said Poulsen, and man did they deliver.

SPOILER ALERT: While I won't be talking about major plot or character spoilers in this story, I do spoil all of the various vehicular scenes and moments, so if you haven't played or finished the game yet, bookmark this page and come back to it later.

The first excellent car moment comes in one of the very first missions, when Bond is going through initial training to join the reborn 00 program. At a facility in Malta, Bond gets thrown the keys to a 2006 Aston Martin DBS, the same car seen in "Casino Royale" and "Quantum of Solace." It's described as "a classic" by one of Bond's compatriots, who adds it's "seen some real-life action too back in her day. Still a gorgeous drive." She's not lying — the DBS is totally banged up, covered in duct tape and zip ties.

Bond proceeds to race around a course, smashing through boxes and jumping down staircases. Despite how realistic the cars look and sound the handling is definitely on the arcade side of things, but it's also very fun. You're encouraged to do handbrake turns and always keep the throttle pinned, and enough actual skill and good driving is needed to do well in this mission and subsequent ones.

It's not a very long sequence, with Bond quickly moving on to other parts of training, but it's a great way to get us car people excited at the beginning of the game. This particular choice of car this early on is a great nod to our outgoing Bond on the silver screen, too. Bond movies have always had car chases, but it wasn't until the Daniel Craig films that the chases really leveled up, becoming grittier and more realistic and more exciting, sometimes the most blockbuster-y set pieces in the entire movie.

A few missions later, Bond and his fellow 00 trainees have to infiltrate a chess tournament at the Grand Carpathian Hotel in Slovakia, and they drive up in a 2007 Jaguar XJ, with Bond dressed as a chauffeur. This is where the game really nails the Bond feeling, with the regular cars and seemingly mundane scenes, and it was this aspect of putting cars in the game that Poulsen and Vega enjoyed the most. "The Bond franchise is not only about the low end and the high end, it's also about the sleeper cars. It's about getting the car people to go, 'I see you,' and it's for us to go, 'we see you too.' The middle of the pack there is really exciting to me," Poulsen said.

"Each of the Bond movies, the best ones, are a cohort of bands. Think of the curation of the cast of vehicles in the movie," Vega added. "We're making an 18-hour experience, we want to make sure that the cast of vehicles gets the nod [from fans]. It gets the 'ah OK, I see what you're doing there, oh you're doing a twist here, oh it's 'Living Daylights.'" The development team was in contact with Aston Martin and Jaguar Land Rover from the very beginning, and they knew they wanted to include a big variety of cars. "There's partnerships that you have to do with Bond, right, it's not Bond without certain vehicles in the mix, but then you still always want to surprise, you want to 'and' the expectation. The player should absolutely have their fantasy met, 'and'. I think it's really nice when people in the car world notice that," he said.

In the Slovakia scene, the player calmly drives the XJ down a winding mountain road toward the hotel. You don't drive fast, or take any shortcuts, or go off the set path; it's all about setting a scene. While driving the XJ, Bond and his crew are talking about the mission and giving you more backstory, like in a transitional movie scene. Instead of making something like this a cutscene, "First Light" lets you act it out. It's like the vehicular equivalent of a "walk and talk," if you know your movie-biz slang. You can take your time on the drive, which is especially nice now that a photo mode has been added to the game. (Everything you see in this story was captured by yours truly.) With how much of the game is straight-up action, it's really nice to have these moments of levity in between that add more depth to the characters.

Poulsen says the development team visited the JLR headquarters in Gaydon, getting tours of the heritage collection and its restoration facility, to figure out what vehicles they might want to use in the game. There was everything from classics to prototypes and rarities like the XJ220, plus things that aren't even out yet. "We walked around discussing, 'hmm, we need something that's not what gets the front page, what is it going to be?' We looked at the XJ of that particular year; it has a story of old money rather than flash, it's not so old that it's vintage, it's just in-between," Poulsen said. They pointed it out to Jaguar, saying "we want that one," and the company obliged. It is a perfect spec, too, one of the later facelifts in green over cream with those lovely five-spoke wheels.

Another part of the game sees Bond and his mentor heading to Mauritania, with the level starting off in a kitted-out Land Rover Defender. You get to drive the SUV across the gorgeous landscape, even doing some light rock crawling, before coming upon a city built inside of a ship graveyard that's run by the pirate king Bawma, played brilliantly by Lenny Kravitz. It's again used as a conversation piece, setting up the scenes to come. Bond gets back in the Defender for another exploring sequence a little later, and after a series of events including a lot more fighting, he gets rescued with the help of a great Series II pickup.

It's not all mundane drives, though. Later in the mission that starts with the XJ, after chaos ensues at the hotel, Bond has to chase after someone who's just hopped into a second-gen Jaguar XKR. He does so by breaking into and hotwiring a Bahama Yellow 1970 Aston Martin DBS, the same model used in "On Her Majesty's Secret Service" (and the same color as the DBS from "The Persuaders"). There's a great moment of Bond cheekily flirting with a mysterious woman who hops into the car with him, and you're off.

Again, it's a pre-set route, but there are a bunch of fun shortcuts like swerving into a muddy field to avoid logging trucks, jumping over canals and driving through a farmer's market. You have to avoid other traffic and contend with other obstacles, and it can actually be a little tough at times. In one climactic moment, Bond drives the car onto and over a transport truck, using it as a ramp to get huge air. Who among us hasn't dreamt of doing that hundreds of times? Adding to the fun is a great damage model, with the DBS getting really beat up by the end of it even if you try and drive safely.

The car chase ends, but Bond is immediately thrust into a firefight at an airfield, which, of course, leads to him dodging explosions to hitch a ride on an escaping cargo plane. To do so, he takes control over one of those airport stair trucks, and jumps onto the wing of the plane. Then he fights a bunch of bad guys on the wing as the plane takes off, and eventually it leads to him freefalling from the plane, avoiding debris while trying to find a parachute — and you're in control pretty much the whole time. It's awesome, and really embodies that unique Bond feeling.

Like the "Hitman" games, "First Light" is all about using your environment to your advantage, and there's a lot of moments where a vehicle is part of that environment. In parts of the game where more baddies are arriving in cars, you can shoot at them, causing them to leak gas or oil and become easier to explode. Some vehicles can be hacked, with Bond able to set off car alarms or remotely activate gadgets like smoke screens. "Plenty of movies you see a car gadget that you never get to see [used in] the movie, so that was a little bit of a head nod there," Vega said.

The two best vehicular sequences in the game don't feature cars at all, though. Or at least not ones you're driving, since you do get to smash up plenty of others. At one point in the game, Bond escapes a gala by commandeering a garbage truck and wreaking havoc through the streets of London, and then later on he takes control of a giant mining truck and drives it through a quarry in Vietnam to, again, save his friends and escape the bad guys. While both sequences are small parts of much longer missions, they're absolute blasts to play through, some of my favorite moments of the game for sure, and they feel totally Bond. Just as you might realize, "oh hell yeah, I think Bond is gonna get in that tank" while watching one of the films, I was sitting on my couch thinking, "oh hell yeah, am I about to drive that thing?"

Vega said the two sequences came about in very different ways, with the mine truck being more organic. "The level's getting put together, and somebody puts a prop in, and then I think there is that incentive in game development, and especially in Bond, where you just want to say 'yes' to the player. Like you're rolling downhill, and someone throws something into the snowball, and instead of rejecting it you keep going." You see a bunch of these mining trucks earlier on in the mission, the game teasing what's about to come without you even knowing it yet. "The mining truck there being part of establishing the space, the place, and then ever so slightly you go, 'guys, well, let's go,' because you embody the mind of the character," Poulsen said of the scene being thought up on the fly.

"[That's the thing of First Light] being a catered experience. It's not open world, it's not everything, it's not all the vehicles; it's a specific subset of vehicles. That means it's interesting for us, that we can lure you forward with this truck and all these things, and have you go 'ah, yeah,' you got what you wanted. Rather than have a plethora of vehicles in front of you and you go 'OK, cool, which one?' No, the one we chose," said Poulsen. "You get what you wanted, but in a way that you wholly couldn't imagine or didn't imagine — unless you're a Bond fan, of course, in which case maybe you do imagine it."

The garbage truck, meanwhile, was a much longer road to get to. Vega talked about how resourcefulness is one of Bond's key character traits, one that differentiates him from other secret agent characters, and that's baked into the level designs from jump. Another one of Bond's best qualities is how he'll smash straight through an obstacle and keep on going. Which he does in this sequence quite a lot. "It was always in the script that he's going to get into this bin lorry in this really unexpected fashion. I think Craig embodied that, I think Brosnan had a bit of that, I think Dalton had a bit of that — put two dots on a map and draw a straight line, that's how James Bond got there, and that is a really cool moment of expression," Vega said.

What makes the garbage truck escape especially cathartic is how the mission starts: with an upset Bond sitting in traffic in a Range Rover SV, having a conversation with his boss. You can't even side-swipe traffic, with the game displaying a parking sensor warning on the screen and pulling the car back into the lane. "It's such a Londoner inside commentary there. He's frustrated, he's been told to stand down. We stick you in a powerful fantasy vehicle like the Land Rover and you can't open it up, you're just running errands. It's putting you in that headspace," said Vega, "so when we finally put you behind the wheel [of the garbage truck] an hour and a half later it's just like go go go go through the wall!"

One thing the game doesn't let Bond drive is what would traditionally be the hero car. In fact, the game rubs it in your face a bit. In a mission fairly early on in the game, you visit Q in his lab and have a conversation in front of a covered-up car that's being worked on by robots. The car is clearly the Aston Martin Valhalla to any slightly trained eye, and Q refuses to let Bond drive it even when it gets finished. He's not even a 00 yet, of course he's not getting to drive Q's souped-up supercar, and it's being built for a different agent anyway. "It kind of puts you in the shoes of Bond," said Vega, "who's like 'I wish you'd give me this on a mission!' That's always Q and Bond's relationship, it's a bit of restraint from Q where he gives you the gadgets you need, he's not giving you the keys to the castle."

Well, Bond will be Bond, so in one of the game's final missions, there's a scene where Bond gets those proverbial castle keys. To escape a siege on Q's lab he climbs into the Valhalla, fires up its machine guns and rockets, and does donuts around the room, killing bad guys and blowing basically everything up. You do actually get to control the car for this scene, but it's pretty brief, leading right into a cutscene that has a few comedic moments involving the car and its built-in voice assistant. It's a satisfying moment, since for hours after the Aston Martin is first seen, you've probably been thinking "man, I really hope we get to do something with the Valhalla."

The team began working on the game a little before the Valhalla was production-ready, receiving the CAD files from Aston so they could get started on it and figure out how to Bond-ify the car. "One of the artistic pillars for the project is that dichotomy between downplayed credibility and outlandish adventurous over-the-top-ness. We need to combine those two things," said Poulsen. Since the game uses a third-person camera view where you're looking forward from behind the character, and thus behind any vehicle, they decided against putting weapons in the headlights or front end of the car, where you wouldn't see them. The Valhalla has such a short, low front end there wasn't much to work with anyway. "We went with a design that would utilize the body and the shapes in an interesting way, and it made sense to put all the weaponry on the back," Poulsen said. "The principal concept artist did a bunch of designs with the CAD model, where we were experimenting with opening up the panels like a beetle or folding them in and out."

When they sent their ideas back to Aston, explaining the why and how behind their designs, the Aston team simply gave them the thumbs up. "Rather than being like 'no actually, you have a radiator there, and this and there's pipes and the thing and the valve,' they didn't give us a hard time because I think they could see that we had tried to some degree respect the negative space and interior space and all that stuff in the car," Poulsen said. The final design features the Valhalla's rear quarter panels opening out horizontally at an angle to reveal a giant auto-aiming machine gun mounted on an arm on each side. "We got that wonderful interplay and a really playful yet rather credible sense of weapons folding out. I'm sure it wouldn't necessarily be doable like this, but [Aston] didn't have a hard time with us wanting to do that, so it was a very smooth relationship."

That couldn't be it for the Valhalla, so the "First Light" team has released a few "Valhalla Protocol" time trial tactical simulation missions as DLC that let you really wring out the Valhalla on a newly designed course, either with or without weapons and obstacles engaged. When you're using the machine guns and rockets to take down obstacles, drones and other vehicles, it's definitely more outlandish than Bond movies usually are, but it fits right in with past Bond video games and reminds me of other excellent titles like "Spy Hunter." Even in strictly time trial mode it's pretty tough to achieve one of the best time targets, since you've still got to contend with all sorts of things on the road, like ramps that you obviously will want to jump off.

"At IO, we believe that shipping the game is the first step. We tell you the story we want to tell, but there's so much more that comes after that. We knew long, long ago that the best possible moment for the Valhalla was the climax of the techo-wizard experience. You've just done all this awesome stuff in Q lab, you see everything that Q built for you, it was always going to be racing around in donuts because you don't want to end that on a chase," Vega said. The final pursuit of the villain is very personal, so a climatic car chase wouldn't have felt right, but because the player now had Valhalla expectations from the start, Vega said they always planned to design post-launch content where you get to actually drive it.

It's a great redemption for the Valhalla, which has had a rough start to its time being featured in the Bond franchise. Aston revealed the concept version of the Valhalla in early 2019, and it originally played a bigger role in Craig's final Bond film, "No Time to Die," which started filming soon after the concept's reveal. That movie was supposed to come out in April 2020, but it ended up being delayed until September 2021 — a couple months after the production version of the Valhalla had been revealed, with a very different design and powertrain compared to the concept. In the final cut of the film, you briefly see the concept sitting in the background of Q's lab, and that's it.

As for what might make it into the next Bond movie, "First Light" could give us a little hint. In one scene when you're being escorted out of that ship graveyard by Bawma, Bond walks right past a bright blue Jaguar Type 00 concept. "Visiting the JLR design studio as a car nerd was just a dream come true, it was so cool, and we got to see the prototype of the Type 00, which was incredible," Poulsen said, "It was impressive to be there and see how that works. Walking past the clay model, it was just cool." JLR has provided Land Rovers and other cars to Bond movies for most of Craig's run, but I'm sure that its heavy involvement with "First Light" is a sign that it wants some new Jaguars to be featured in the next movies. I mean come on, can't you see James Bond driving the production Type 01? At least someone should, whether it's a villain or a Bond girl or hell, even M.

I couldn't get off the phone without asking Poulsen and Vega to tell me their favorite vehicular moments in the Bond film series, whether it be a major chase or a more nerdy throwaway moment. "I want to give a shoutout to the start of 'Quantum of Solace' just because it's so visceral and moody and cool," Poulsen said. "It's not as fun as the fun ones, it's more violent and hardcore, but damn that flows well. Or rather, it doesn't flow, it's much too staccato to flow, but ooh it's so good."

That is a great answer, but Vega holds the correct opinion, because his favorite is also my favorite. "Mine is 'GoldenEye,' but it's also because I think the dialogue plays such a role. It's a car chase where the villain is the Bond girl, but she's not having the conversation with him, he's having the conversation with the girl in the car," Vega said, referring to Bond's passenger, an MI6 employee named Caroline who is supposed to be psychologically investigating him. "I think it's the interplay; it's like a gunfight and it's a gadget fight and it's a car chase, all with just dialogue and driving. And it's low stakes, it's just having fun on the mountains. It's such an homage to the Bond girl outrunning Bond in a casual environment."

That's just one of a few great chases in that film. "The scale is so impressive, and it's the same thing [as the game] right, you go from high-precision handling, the fantasy of being the most capable person in the world, to driving a tank through a wall," Vega said, referring to one of "GoldenEye's" best moments. "I love the goofy vehicles of Bond a lot," he added. "I love the gondolas and I love the hovercrafts. It just brings out the boyish spectacle that makes me giggle."

Boyish spectacle is the best way to put it, and that's what "First Light" really gets right. Yes, the cars and traditional chase scenes are excellent, but what makes this game feel so undeniably Bond are the unexpected moments. We've got the holidays coming up, and you've got a week before GTA VI comes out, so if you haven't played through "007 First Light" yet, there's no better time to do so than the present. Though I guess you've got all the time in the world.

#5661. Recapping the headwinds and tailwinds from Q3

Recapping the headwinds and tailwinds from Q3" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

For-profit payers and providers have wrapped up yet another round of earnings calls. Editor Dave Muoio, Senior Reporter Paige Minemyer and Reporter Cailey Gleeson sit down to discuss the key takeaways from third-quarter earnings reports.

#5660. Is Tesla The Apple Of The EV Industry?

Tesla is an interesting company at an interesting phase of the electric vehicle transition. Well, it has always been an interesting company in this transition. However, lately, as the industry has moved quickly into a new phase, I have been wondering where Tesla will sit amidst it in the coming years.

While there are now hundreds and hundreds of electric vehicle models on the market and EV share of new vehicle sales have risen to 32% globally (or 23% if we only count full electrics/BEVs), Tesla sells only two models but remains the second best selling BEV producer in the world. It remains solidly ahead of other automakers and only trails Chinese giant BYD (or also Geely if we throw in PHEVs).

In the US, where there’s much less EV competition (especially because of Chinese EVs basically being blocked from entering the market), Tesla’s share of EV sales is above 50%.

The Apple iPhone holds about 20–22% of the global smartphone market, and around 58% of the US smartphone market. While that’s a bit higher than Tesla’s shares, it’s quite similar. And considering that Apple just sells that one phone (but in a few different versions) and Tesla just sells the Model 3 and Model Y, it’s hard to not see a comparison.

As EV sales have been picking up in parts of the world, or at least reaching portions of the market in the USA, it seems to me that a lot of people choose Tesla because they’ve heard it’s the EV brand. Tesla has long been the face of the EV transition in most of the world, and even considered the only “real” option. I know of many buyers in the US who just went straight to Tesla without looking at other options. It’s a bit like how many people will only consider and look at Apple for phones. However, how much will that change in the coming years? Will Tesla’s dominance fade further (it’s already down from about 80% of the market in the US to a bit more than 50%) as more and more options come to market, or will it hold it’s special position as a first mover, a tech leader, and an EV-only carmaker?

One more interesting issue here is the matter of “Full Self Driving” — which only comes as “Supervised” at the moment, but Tesla is itching to make a fully hands-off, eyes-off option for Tesla owners. Could Tesla achieve a leading role for years again of having the only fully hands-off, eyes-off, self-driving consumer cars on the road? Or is it not going to beat other automakers to market with a self-driving feature that takes liability for any accidents away from the human operating the car and allows them to sleep, watch movies, work, etc. in the car? This has been hotly debated for about a decade, with Tesla nearly a decade late on Elon Musk’s targets for this, but the company is now operating robotaxis in a few cities and ramping up production of the Cybercab. Risky or not, is Tesla close to taking on liability for humans using FSD in their own cars? And if it does take that step, will that make demand for Tesla cars so high that Tesla sees its EV market share increase significantly again?

I know many people have strong opinions on these matters — on both sides of the debate. Some are certain Tesla is going to rise again and will release true Full Self Driving broadly soon, or at least long before other automakers. Some are certain Tesla’s vision-only approach is fatally flawed and Tesla will never take on full liability for Tesla owners using FSD. I don’t have enough insight or expertise to say one thing is absolutely certain and the other is not, but I do think the FSD matter is quite important to Tesla remaining “the Apple of the EV industry” — even potentially without taking on accident liability. Even with things staying as they are, I wonder if Tesla could remain the dominant EV player in the US and some other markets just because of its early reputation, branding, and 21st century marketing. We shall see.

Zach is tryin' to help society help itself one word at a time. He spends most of his time here on CleanTechnica as its editor-in-chief and CEO. Zach is recognized globally as an electric vehicle, solar energy, and energy storage expert. He has presented about electric vehicles and renewable energy at conferences in India, the UAE, Ukraine, Poland, Germany, the Netherlands, the USA, Canada, and Curaçao.

#5659. FAA's Nationwide Ground Stop Definitely Not Because Of New AI Air Traffic Control Tool... Probably

At 3:45 p.m. ET on Friday afternoon, the Federal Aviation Administration issued a nationwide ground stop, halting all flights that were set to take off from U.S. airports until further notice. An ATCSCC advisory was posted by Flightradar24 on Bluesky, which reads: "Due to extraordinary circumstances and for reasons of safety. Attention all aircraft operators, by order of the Federal Aviation Command Center all airports are ground stopped. All departing traffic should remain ground stopped until further notice. Inbound international flights will be managed on a case by case basis." Some international flights have been allowed to take off, as well. The initial announcement stated the ground stop is scheduled to stay in effect until 11:00 p.m. ET, but it seems like it'll last longer than that. (Flightradar has an ongoing live blog here.)

In a Twitter post a short time later, Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said, "The FAA issued a nationwide ground stop for all U.S. airports following a communication issue impacting all Air Route Traffic Control Centers. FAA technicians are investigating the issue. We will provide immediate updates as we have them." According to News9 Oklahoma, reports from aviation monitoring account OSINTdefender said the FAA's ground stop is due to communication frequency issues affecting the National Airspace System. In a short post, the FAA said, "There has been a temporary telecommunications outage – out of an abundance of caution all flights have been halted. Every flight has been accounted for and international flights will be handled on a case by case basis." As of 6:45 p.m., President Trump has yet to make any official statement.

In about as funny a turn of events as you could possibly imagine, our team found out about this situation from a social media post by Flavor Flav, who seems to be one of the first people to post about it. This is a developing story, and we'll update you as we learn more.

A scan of the National Airspace System Status website shows major airports listed as "airport closure," "ground stop," "ground delay," or "departure delay." The last time the FAA enacted a ground stop was in January 2023, when all domestic departures were paused for less than two hours because of a pilot notification system went down. Before that, the last ground stop had been on September 11, 2001.

A few days ago we told you about how the FAA is getting rid of ATC's floppy disks, and just yesterday it started rolling out a new AI air traffic tool. While there's nothing concrete to suggest the AI system had anything to do with the outage, Secretary of Homeland Security Markwayne Mullin stood in front of some cameras to answer questions from the media, and he didn't outright deny that AI had anything to do with it making sure to say "we call it SI, remember," and that "we can't rule out anything at this point." He did say there are no indications of the outages being caused by a cyberattack or terror threat, but when asked again whether AI caused the problem, he said, "we're looking at that, that's part of something that obviously through CISA at DHS we're taking a look, we're working with our counterparts at all agencies."

In a tweet, ASI, the contractor who implemented that new AI system, said, "The current ground stop is not based on or a result of the SMART system, any product of ASI or our integration with FAA and ATC." As of 6:45 p.m. ET, the latest info comes from sources that spoke to CBS News: "an update to the private communications network that connects America's air traffic control system caused the system to reboot, leading to a crash and a communications outage." That system is operated by L3Harris.

Mullin also said, "I don't want to say that this is a crisis, but it is, because we had to do a nationwide ground stoppage," and that Duffy is doing "a phenomenal job in crisis mode." When asked if it will be days or weeks before the problem is resolved, Mullin said it's too early to tell. Great!

Our very own Andy Kalmowitz is caught up in this mess, he was about to take off from Chicago O'Hare International Airport on a connecting flight while on his way home from driving the new Subaru Forester Wilderness Hybrid. Right as they were getting ready to taxi the ground stop was issued, and his pilot came over the intercom and explained the situation: "We're in a nationwide ground stop right now. That's not ground delay, that's ground stop. Basically the FAA has said, 'look, we're having radio problems at multiple centers across the nation; it's not just United Airlines, and it's not just Chicago. If you're airborne, that's great, you're coming in to land,' but they are not allowing anyone else to take off and get out there into the system. Which is understandable, but I don't like it, and I know you don't either." On CNN, Pete Muntean said, "this large scale radio frequency outage has caused pilots to resort to the emergency frequency that pilots use as a last resort."

#5658. Response to Standard PBC Treatment at 1 Year Linked to Lower Long-Term Risks

— "Response predicts outcomes in real-world care, and nonresponders carry a risk," researcher says

A biochemical response to ursodeoxycholic acid (UDCA) at 12 months was associated with lower 5-year risks of hepatic decompensation and mortality in patients with primary biliary cholangitis (PBC), a propensity-matched landmark cohort study suggested.

Among patients considered to be responders -- defined as an alkaline phosphatase (ALP) level ≤130 U/L and a bilirubin level ≤1.0 mg/dL -- the 5-year cumulative incidence of hepatic decompensation was 10.2% versus 17.8% among nonresponders (HR 0.60, 95% CI 0.51-0.70, P<0.001), reported EzzElDien A. Ibrahim, MD, MSc, of the University of Missouri in Columbia, at the American Association for the Study of Liver Diseases annual meeting in Denver.

In addition, the cumulative incidence of 5-year all-cause mortality was 12.2% among responders versus 16.3% for nonresponders (HR 0.74, 95% CI 0.65-0.84, P<0.001).

Other clinical outcomes also trended in favor of or significantly favored responders:

"Response predicts outcomes in real-world care, and nonresponders carry a risk," Ibrahim said. "We can check ALP and bilirubin at 1 year on UDCA," and at that point, clinicians should consider "escalating nonresponders without delay."

UDCA is the first-line treatment for PBC, a rare and chronic autoimmune disease of the bile ducts, "and it ... works," Ibrahim noted.

An international cohort study that included 3,902 patients from the Global PBC Study Group database showed that after inverse probability treatment weighting, the 10-year cumulative liver transplant-free survival rate was 79.7% among UDCA-treated patients and 60.7% among untreated patients, with UDCA treatment resulting in a statistically significantly reduced risk of liver transplant or death (HR 0.46, 95% CI 0.40-0.52, P<0.001).

However, 20% to 40% of patients do not respond biochemically to UDCA at 12 months. In 2024, seladelpar (Livdelzi) and elafibranor (Iqirvo) were granted accelerated approval for patients with PBC who either did not respond to UDCA or were intolerant to the treatment.

While a number of criteria such as the PARIS criteria link 12-month response to UDCA to outcomes, Ibrahim said real-world validation in routine care for 5-year clinical endpoints -- such as decompensation and death -- is limited.

This explains "why this study matters," he noted.

For this study, Ibrahim and colleagues used the TriNetX U.S. Research Network that includes data on 147.5 million patients from 112 health systems.

They included matched cohorts of 2,911 patients with PBC. Mean age was 61.3, 87% were women, 77% were white, 34% had a cirrhosis code, 14% had portal hypertension, and mean albumin level was 3.86-3.91 g/dL.

Responders and nonresponders to UDCA were categorized based on propensity score matching of 29 covariates that included demographics, liver and mental health comorbidities, alanine aminotransferase levels, aspartate aminotransferase levels, platelet counts, albumin levels, and international normalized ratio.

Ibrahim acknowledged that the study had a number of limitations, including the fact that PBC was defined by codes and not by a positive anti-mitochondrial antibody test or histology. In addition, there was no access to UDCA dose or adherence data, and there were a limited number of 5-year events for the liver transplant and hepatocellular carcinoma analyses.

#5657. CareSource launches $1M grant program to support AI-powered solutions

CareSource launches $1M grant program to support AI-powered solutions" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Nonprofit insurer CareSource is launching a $1 million program that aims to back responsible AI solutions in healthcare.

The initiative, launched through the CareSource Foundation, is seeking proposals for ways to advance and identify deployments for AI that can drive better outcomes and address barriers for individuals enrolled in Medicaid or who otherwise struggle to access care.

The program will provide up to $1 million over the course of two years to ensure that the tool can deliver on meaningful and scalable benefits for the communities it serves.

Erhardt Preitauer, CEO of CareSource, said in an email to Fierce Healthcare that AI offers the potential to improve not just patient care but the experience for providers too as they navigate complex conditions.

"I'm a big believer that when technology, healthcare and the people in our communities rally around a shared purpose, magic can happen," said Preitauer. "Leaving no stone unturned is the only way we can make healthcare work better for people, and AI gives us the power to explore every possibility."

The program will include two phases. First, interested applicants can submit shorter proposals, with the goal of generating interest. Applications that are selected to move on will be tasked with submitting a more fleshed-out proposal, including details on implementation, AI governance and sustainability.

CareSource is aiming to identify solutions that are ready for implementation and has the potential for scale. Potential focus areas include care coordination, member engagement, behavioral health and rural healthcare. Solutions should use AI to support care delivery, but it should not replace human judgement or oversight, the insurer said.

To be eligible, a qualified nonprofit organization must be leading the effort, according to the announcement. Technology companies, academic researchers, healthcare organizations and community-based groups can also play a role as partners.

#5656. Michelle Yeoh Says Window Seats Are Underrated, And She's Right (That You Should Leave The Aisle Seats For Me)

By definition, hot takes are supposed to be controversial. If everyone agrees with your hot take, it's probably a lukewarm take at best, because that's how kids decided the slang worked. Kareem Rahma has taken advantage of this with Subway Takes, a show where he sits on the New York subway and records people's hot takes. This week, Oscar-winning actress Michelle Yeoh made an appearance and decided the hill she would die on is that window seats are underrated. A lot of people won't like it, but you know what? She's right.

Flying is a miracle of engineering and modern manufacturing. We literally built machines that defy the will of the gods, zoom across our skies, and let us travel in ways we were never meant to travel. Should I have been able to pull off a whirlwind visit to Boston while living in Georgia? Probably not, but the miracle of flight made it possible, and there's always going to be some thing special about sitting in a window seat that lets you see the beautiful world beneath you. Los Angeles isn't the prettiest city in the world, but even LA looks incredible from 10,000 or 15,000 feet.

With the window seat, you'll also have an easier time napping on your flight, and air travel takes so much time out of your day, you could probably use a snooze. Yes, most seats now have those bendy headrests that are supposed to make napping a little easier, but nothing beats using a sweater as a pillow and leaning against the plane. The bendy headrests can try, but while they're better than nothing, they still don't work all that great. All humans need to sleep, so book a window seat, enjoy the view, and then take a nice nap once the plane makes it above the clouds.

Does going to the bathroom get a little awkward? Sure, but there's no reason you have to drink four whiskey cokes before you board your flight. They have edibles for that now. Any time you get up to go to the bathroom, that's also the opportunity to make a new friend. Most importantly, if everyone who reads this ends up fighting over those beautiful views from the window, there will be more aisle seats available for me to take. Michelle may have a point, but I still prefer the aisle and will not be changing my mind.

#5655. Trump Officials Will Fund New Research on Controversial Psychedelic Drug Ibogaine

— Veterans groups, psychedelic advocates say the drug has great promise for mental heath conditions

Federal officials on Thursday announced new funding to explore the medical potential of ibogaine, a controversial psychedelic drug championed by veterans, conservative lawmakers, and podcasters aligned with President Donald Trump.

Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. said his department is awarding up to $4.9 million to prepare for the first U.S. studies of ibogaine for post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD), a hard-to-treat condition linked to higher suicide and substance abuse rates among veterans.

The money will not go directly to patient research, Kennedy said, but to lay the groundwork needed to manufacture, dose, and design studies of the drug.

Ibogaine is made from a shrub native to West Africa. It can cause intense hallucinations, nausea, vomiting, tremors, and sometimes deadly irregular heart rhythms. Ibogaine is currently illegal, in the same ultra-restrictive class as LSD, ecstasy, and most other psychedelic drugs.

Veterans organizations and psychedelic advocates contend that ibogaine has great promise for intractable conditions such as PTSD, severe depression, and opioid addiction. U.S. research on the drug has essentially been frozen for decades due to ibogaine's restricted status.

Kennedy said the government will receive data and treatment guidance from Beond Inc., a Texas-based company that has treated more than 3,000 patients with ibogaine in Mexico, where the drug is not illegal.

"That is going to allow us to dramatically accelerate the clinical trial process," Kennedy said. "We're going to have, I think, a record drug approval process."

Additional funding awards may explore ibogaine's use for addiction, he said.

Veterans Affairs Secretary Doug Collins, who joined Kennedy for the announcement, said the collaborative effort would focus first on safety and "best-in-class research."

"For veterans living with PTSD, we owe them both urgency and the best science we can produce," Collins said.

Kennedy and Collins have previously voiced support for the potential of psychedelics, citing the experiences of veterans who have traveled abroad for therapy with ibogaine and other mind-altering substances.

Thursday's announcement was previewed in April, when Trump signed an executive order directing federal agencies to speed up review and research of psychedelics that could help with mental health conditions.

Three applications for psychedelics are pending review at the FDA. They include the first potential medicines made from psilocybin, the active ingredient in magic mushrooms, and MDMA, also called ecstasy. Decisions on those drugs could come within weeks or months.

Because of its safety risks, ibogaine research has moved more slowly.

The little data currently available mostly come from patients who have traveled to Mexico or other countries where ibogaine is not heavily regulated. Ibogaine clinics typically charge $10,000 to $20,000 for one course of treatment, which often involves monitoring of a patient's heart readings while emergency medical workers are on standby.

Trump's order on psychedelics came after podcaster Joe Rogan said he texted Trump about ibogaine, which he repeatedly has discussed on his show. According to Rogan, the president quickly responded: "Sounds great. Do you want FDA approval? Let's do it."

#5654. Carelon agrees to pay $10M to settle ghost network, coverage denial allegations in New York

Carelon agrees to pay $10M to settle ghost network, coverage denial allegations in New York" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Carelon will pay more than $10 million across two settlements in New York over mental health networks and coverage denials.

The company, a division of Elevance Health, will pay $2.35 million in penalties, fees and costs as well as up to $8 million in restitution for members who paid out-of-pocket for out-of-network mental healthcare, to reimburse those whose claims were improperly denied or to overhaul its networks.

New York Attorney General Letitia James said in an announcement that the settlements resolve two investigations from her office that determined the company misled members by directing them to seek care from providers that were out-of-network—through so-called "ghost networks"—and improperly denied coverage for treatment for substance abuse disorder.

James noted that Carelon administers behavioral health benefits for multiple plans, including Anthem Blue Cross, EmblemHealth, Independent Health and the Empire Plan, which covers state and local government workers.

Across these insurers, Carelon manages behavioral health benefits for more than 4 million people in the state, James said.

The alleged behavior also goes against a 2015 agreement with the AG's office, where the company agreed that it would maintain accurate provider networks and "use approved criteria" when denying substance abuse treatments.

"When someone is ready to seek help for a mental health condition or addiction, every day matters," said James. “Carelon left New Yorkers calling provider after provider who could not see them and made it harder for people to get the treatment to which they were entitled."

"These settlements will help people get the care they need and put money back in New Yorkers’ pockets," she said.

Elevance Health said in a statement to Fierce Healthcare that it is committed to ensuring patients have access to the most up-to-date provider information.

"Carelon Behavioral Health is committed to helping people find and access high-quality mental health and substance use disorder care. Accurate, up-to-date provider information is an important part of that effort," the company said. "We continue to invest in tools and processes that support accurate provider information and work closely with providers and other partners to help connect consumers with the care they need."

Carelon was hit with a lawsuit in April over the alleged ghost networks and their impact on the Empire Plan in particular. In the case, plaintiff's attorneys conducted secret shopper calls to analyze Carelon's network, calling 300 providers. Just 17% of those contacted took insurance and were open to new patients.

These "ghost networks" have also drawn concern in other markets, such as for maternal care services in Medicaid and in Medicare Advantage. A study released last month found that commercial plans also routinely have inaccurate provider directories.

#5653. Europe’s Hydrogen Strategy Should Begin With the Industrial Customer

A European fertiliser manufacturer buys ammonia. Its immediate concern is getting the right product, at the right quality and price, with reliable deliveries. The manufacturer may produce ammonia using hydrogen made on its own site, buy hydrogen through a pipeline, or import ammonia made somewhere else. The last choice preserves much of the downstream industrial work but creates no demand for additional hydrogen production or pipelines inside Europe. That distinction belongs at the start of Europe’s hydrogen strategy.

The European Commission has reopened the strategy for consultation, with submissions due November 23. The Commission acknowledges that renewable and low-carbon hydrogen production and use have grown more slowly than expected. Europe’s public investment decisions now need a closer look at the industries expected to use hydrogen, the products they make, and the routes by which those products can be supplied competitively. The consultation is open until November 23, 2026.

I submitted a demand-led assessment to the Commission on November 11. My TFIE Strategy Briefing explains the industrial argument, and the open research report documents the model and its assumptions.

Europe’s existing hydrogen market is primarily a market for industrial chemicals. ACER estimated EU consumption of about 7.4 million tons in 2024, including 4.2 million tons for petroleum refining and 1.9 million tons for ammonia. Passenger cars, building heat and power generation account for very little of that established market. Refining faces structural decline as petroleum demand changes, while fertiliser and chemical producers can keep manufacturing products even when some upstream inputs come from elsewhere.

Steelmaking also has choices that cannot be captured by a single hydrogen-demand target. Producers can use recycled scrap, purchase reduced iron made where electricity is cheaper, or use hydrogen-based direct reduction where its economics work. The equipment, feedstock quality and production location determine the actual hydrogen requirement. Industrial heat offers direct electrical options for many duties. Transport electrification cuts the amount of liquid fuel that must be produced in the first place. These choices determine hydrogen demand long before anyone chooses a pipeline route.

Electricity costs expose why the industrial comparison matters. Electrolysis consuming 55 kWh/kg uses €3.30 of electricity per kilogram at €60/MWh, and €5.50 at €100/MWh. Those prices exclude the electrolyser, finance, maintenance, compression and delivery. An industrial customer pays for the finished product, not for the complexity of the process used to manufacture it.

The new assessment compares qualifying green ammonia imported into Europe at modelled 2050 delivered costs of €755–839/t with European ammonia made using on-site hydrogen at about €1,222/t. The €383–467/t gap puts a substantial price on choosing the domestic route in this model. Emissions certification, quality, reliability and supply concentration belong in the procurement appraisal, alongside the cost and strategic value of retaining European upstream production. They do not turn a forecast of ammonia demand into a forecast of domestic hydrogen demand.

Industrial policy has reasons to support European production beyond the lowest immediate price. Domestic facilities provide employment, technical capabilities and supply security. The right decision is to identify those benefits, calculate the extra cost and state who will pay it. Hydrogen production quotas bury that choice in a volume target and risk making European industrial products less competitive.

The infrastructure consequence follows. A pipeline needs industrial customers who can afford delivered hydrogen. A capacity reservation or a project announcement provides neither a binding purchase agreement nor a business case for long-term utilisation. Germany’s hydrogen-network reservations and the Netherlands’ lower demand projections already make that difference worth examining. Europe should assess its planned networks against the industrial customers likely to remain after process and sourcing choices change.

European network planners already run scenarios covering industrial activity, technology and hydrogen supply. My November submission proposes adding direct competition between ways of supplying equivalent industrial products and transport services, including the location of production. The resulting hydrogen requirements then become inputs to infrastructure appraisal instead of predetermined outputs of hydrogen policy.

The next step is practical. Keep a proposed pipeline’s design, cost, capacity and planned opening date unchanged. Assess that same investment first under a policy-aligned case that includes existing hydrogen mandates, and then under a separate economic-competition case in which industrial buyers can select other emissions-qualified production routes. Publish projected utilisation, binding customers, delivered costs, tariffs and public exposure. After seeing the downside in the original proposal, compare smaller pipes, staged construction, local hydrogen supply or imported industrial inputs. Redesigning the pipeline at the outset of every scenario hides the risk of the investment actually under consideration.

The accompanying research uses a reproducible Python model, an evidence workbook and an interactive dashboard. Readers can change assumptions about industrial imports, technology and production costs and see how the modelled hydrogen requirement responds. In the Strong pathway with central costs, selected European industrial markets and transport services require 1.887 million tons of physical hydrogen in 2050. Halving the assumed import capacity for industrial products raises that result to 3.206 million tons. These figures describe selected markets and exclude hydrogen embodied in imported finished products; they are not a forecast of total EU demand.

A competitive industrial economy can still need hydrogen for chemicals and some other applications. It needs production and pipelines sized for customers, with any extra capacity supported by a stated security or industrial rationale. The Commission’s renewed strategy should begin with Europe’s industrial products and services, then calculate the hydrogen system worth paying for.

Read the original TFIE Strategy Briefing, the open European hydrogen assessment, and explore the interactive dashboard, evidence workbook and reproducible Python model.

#5652. 32 Hospitalized After Asphalt Tanker Slams Into And Sinks Docked Riverboat In New Orleans

The Mississippi River is partially closed after a tanker ship t-boned and sank a docked riverboat with approximately 129 passengers and crew members on board in the Port of New Orleans at approximately 12:30 p.m. ET on Friday. City officials said an estimated 32 people have been transported to nearby hospitals with non-critical injuries, and all passengers are now accounted for, miraculously confirming no casualties from the tragedy. The exact number of injuries caused in the collision is not currently known, as it remains an active and evolving incident. The collision happened outside 1350 Port of New Orleans Place, and the U.S. Coast Guard closed the Mississippi River to all vessel traffic between mile markers 93 and 96.

The American Cruise Lines' American Heritage riverboat was docked for about two hours to begin passenger disembarkation when the Atlantic Asphalt tanker collided with it. WDSU News said, "Port NOLA Harbor Police, the New Orleans Fire Department, the New Orleans Police Department, the U.S. Coast Guard and emergency medical personnel are on scene, conducting rescue and response operations." Their rapid response is being called "a miracle on the Mississippi."

Louisiana Lt. Gov. Billy Nungesser told ABC News he was worried about the victims because these type of river cruises tend to draw elderly passengers. WDSU said rescued passengers are being brought into the Port of New Orleans Administration Building for triage and assistance. The U.S. Army Corps of Engineers said the water temperature near the shipwreck is about 66 degrees, which is cold enough to potentially cause hypothermia if a person is exposed to it for too long.

There is no confirmed cause of the crash, though it is suspected to be due to a loss of steering. After the initial impact, the riverboat began taking on water and capsized very rapidly, and it is now resting on its side at the dock. Louisiana Gov. Jeff Landry rushed to the scene and said he spoke with President Donald Trump about the crash, who told him he had any support he needed. The U.S. Coast Guard is now working on reopening the river, and it is working with salvage assistance teams to salvage the vessel and remove it from the area.

#5650. BJC Health, St. Luke's reveal plans to merge

BJC Health, St. Luke's reveal plans to merge" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Missouri-based BJC Health and St. Luke's Hospital are planning a merger.

The providers announced on Wednesday that they have signed a letter of intent to come together, bringing the community hospital into the fold with the Show Me State's largest health system. The pair expects the deal to improve access and improve local care in the St. Louis region.

St. Luke's said in the announcement that the letter "follows a deliberate and thorough evaluation process," conducted by its board of directors and leaders "to identify the best path forward for St. Luke’s and the communities it serves."

The providers said they intend to spend the next several months building a definitive agreement to merge and complete the needed regulatory approval process. In the interim, St. Luke's patients can continue to see their regular physicians.

BJC said in the announcement that it is committed to supporting local, community care while also allowing St. Luke's to tap into the resources available across its ecosystem. It intends to provide key support for the hospital's clinical technology and equipment as well as backing infrastructure and facility upgrades and provider recruitment and retention efforts.

St. Luke’s is a well-respected hospital, known for clinical quality, patient experience and the personal attention given to everyone in our care,” said Andy Bagnall, CEO of St. Luke’s, in the announcement. “At the same time, the demands of health care delivery and our communities’ needs are changing rapidly."

We believe that together with BJC, we can strengthen patient care in St. Louis in ways we could not easily do on our own," Bagnall said.

Nick Barto, CEO of BJC Health, called St. Luke's "a trusted leader in our region with a reputation for excellence."

"Together, we will build on that foundation through our aligned clinical strengths and complementary services," Barto said in the announcement. "Our shared goal is to expand access to differentiated, high-quality care while ensuring St. Luke’s can continue serving the growing communities of West St. Louis for generations to come.”

#5649. 1970 Dodge Challenger R/T For Sale With 96,199 Miles Shown Wraps 440 V8 In Plum Crazy Purple

It wasn't that long ago that a different, clean, Plum Crazy 1970 Dodge Charger caught our eye on the used market, but there's now a Challenger R/T from the same year and painted the same color available. Apparently, we like Purple around here. Spotted on Hemmings Classified is a 1970 Dodge Challenger R/T with 96,199 miles on the odometer. The seller, Ryle's Rides in Sacramento, California, is asking $69,995 for it.

Per the factory fender tag, this one was originally powered by a 383-cubic-inch Magnum V8 engine paired to a four-speed manual gearbox. But somewhere along the way, somebody dropped a 440 big-block V8 from 1969 into it instead.

#5648. Ask a Scientist: Who Has a Voice in Clean Air Protections?

If developers and planners decide to site an industrial chemical plant next door to your home, you’d want the chance to speak up about it. Soliciting input from the communities and individuals affected by pollution is an essential part of how environmental and public health laws are enforced. Under the federal Clean Air Act, large polluting facilities like power plants, chemical manufacturers, and refineries need to have a building permit, and every permitting decision must go through a “notice and comment” process. Although this is a federal law, it’s largely implemented by state, regional, and local agencies. And as a new UCS analysis shows, how much influence people have on the air permitting process varies greatly depending on where they live.

Darya Minovi leads fair access research for the Center for Science and Democracy at UCS. She is the lead author of Community Access to Clean Air Decisionmaking: Analysis of State Engagement in Title V Permitting, a new report that looks state-by-state at how people can make their voices heard.

AAS: What inspired you to do this analysis?

Darya Minovi: Last year, our team did an analysis of how the Trump administration is limiting public input on regulations andalso developed a set of recommendations for how government agencies should ensure the public gets to participate. With the administration very clearly side-stepping or ignoring these public processes, we did not, to put it lightly, expect the federal government to be a willing recipient of our research and guidance.

During the Biden administration, the White House Office of Management and Budget (OMB) did a lot of work to assess and expand meaningful public participation in government decisions. That was essentially all thrown away when President Trump was inaugurated. After consulting with colleagues and partners, we wanted to know: in this vacuum where the federal government isn’t interested in making progress, could we work with states to advance the public’s ability to participate in environmental policies?

AAS: Why does this participation matter, and how did you end up focusing on the air pollution permitting process?

DM: When you look at the core principles of environmental justice, meaningful engagement is central. People have the right to understand what’s happening in their communities and ideally be part of shaping those decisions. They need the opportunity to speak out and be respectfully listened to. People’s lived experience is expertise that can and should inform policy decisions. That’s democracy in action. At the federal level, environmental justice has been targeted and dismantled, but some states still value these

One specific environmental program where public participation is relevant is in Title V of the federal Clean Air Act (CAA), which covers operating permits for facilities that generate air pollution. This includes power plants, refineries, trash incinerators, and factories that manufacture chemicals—under the law, these are often called “major sources,” and there are very specific legal requirements about public participation. It’s one of the main ways air pollution gets regulated. But even though CAA is a federal law, it’s largely state and local agencies that carry out this permit process.

When a facility owner or company wants to apply for a permit to operate, they must submit a permit application to the relevant state, regional, or local agency. The agency writes a draft permit, which must be posted publicly in some way. The CAA requires agencies to accept public comments on the draft permit for at least 30 days and, if requested by the public, hold a hearing. Agencies also have a legal obligation to respond to substantive comments. These are very clear steps laid out in the law. It’s a high bar for participation, but we wanted to better understand how it works in practice.

What we found in our analysis is that the agencies with the authority to issue permits will approve Title V air permits unless there’s some clear problem with legal compliance. But the public can use that “notice and comment” process to slow down and influence what the facility will actually be permitted to do.

However, some agencies treat the public comment process as a set of boxes to check off. The law isn’t specific about meaningful engagement practices beyond public comment, so our analysis also looked at practices, programs, guidance, and laws at the state level, beyond what’s required by the federal government to enhance public participation in air permitting.

AAS: How did you do this analysis, and what did you find?

DM: We wanted to get the answers to three big questions.

We started with what I’ll call “desktop research”—reviewing agency websites and laws as though we were residents looking to navigate the process. All 50 states do have at least a website with some information. In general, agencies are posting draft permits and accepting public comments, as the CAA requires. But there are big differences in how that happens.

Public hearings aren’t very frequent and usually happen only if there’s a critical mass of people demanding them. We also reviewed nearly 90 permits from 35 different states—the public notices are often very technical, not easily readable by a layperson, and they’ll say things like “comments are due 30 days from posting date” instead of giving the actual dated deadline.

Very few states translate these notices into languages other than English. And we assessed whether the notices use “plain language” best practices—defining scientific terms, using white space and bullet points—and found that most states aren’t integrating these effectively. Minnesota and Michigan stand out as providing clear, readable information, and that’s largely the result of advocacy by local groups there.

We found that while 42 states had some resources describing how people can participate in permitting, the quality varied significantly. By and large permitting webpages and resources seem better designed for regulated industries, not the general public.

Ultimately, a public notice is only useful if you can find it, read it, and understand what it means and what you’re supposed to do to get involved!

We also wanted to know: do the comments make a difference? If they’re just going to get collected and sit on a shelf, that’s less meaningful than if agencies answer them or make actual changes to the permit in response. It’s very rare for agencies to deny a permit, but there are ways for public comments to have an impact—correcting errors, adding monitoring requirements, and strengthening enforcement.

AAS: You reached out to states to learn more. What did you find?

DM: Talking directly to these state agencies was an important part of our research. We built an 88-question survey, based on our September 2025 guidance, to ask agencies how they provide information, what they do to encourage participation, and what resources they have to carry the work out.

While we sent that survey to every state, only half the states responded in full, with 3 more responding partially. A few states said they intended to respond but didn’t, while others declined to participate or failed to respond entirely. That itself speaks to the capacity and, perhaps, interest these agencies have.

The states’ answers varied. Of the 28 states we heard back from, only 18 said they post responses to comments online, and only 13 could give us an example of when public comments resulted in a change to a permit.

At least 11 states have laws that go beyond what’s required in the CAA, and those laws often center environmental justice or cumulative impacts. States with these laws tended to have more readily available information on how to participate, as well as more resources and funding devoted to public outreach. Having these laws—or at least guidelines—in place does seem to create agency-wide changes to enhance public engagement.

Meaningful engagement takes time, money, and actual staff to manage the process. Only 20 of the agencies we spoke to provide training on public participation or environmental justice for staff, and only 16 have actual full-time staff dedicated to the process.

Unfortunately, the federal government is pushing in the opposite direction. Aaron Szabo, the (now former) director of the air-quality office at the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency, issued a memo urging states to expedite the Title V permitting process and rapidly review permits—saying explicitly that agencies don’t get many comments, so they don’t really matter.

DM: I was surprised by how much variability there was between and even within states. Some might have a lot of resources explaining how to participate in air permitting, but it’s a confusing website. Others might translate public notices in other languages but have no other information about how to participate.

While there’s a lot of room for improvement, there are some instructive examples too. For example, North Dakota has explicit guidance on how to write an effective public comment, with examples of common mistakes to avoid. Connecticut and Wisconsin have specific “how to” webpages to help people make comments or request a hearing. South Carolina has a webpage specifically entitled “Air Quality Decisions” that lays out what decision was made and posts the comments made in response to the draft permit. Ohio was the only state that got an “A” grade for readability of a public notice, with most states falling in the B/C range.

Every state can do better, of course, and we have a set of recommendations for how these agencies can improve. Governors, state lawmakers, and agency heads have a role to play here—certainly with funding for agencies and laws, but also in how they direct their agencies to carry this process out. Using public health data, translating notices into culturally appropriate languages and presenting them in plain language, making sure there’s fulltime staff devoted to public participation—these are just some of the steps to make participating in the air permitting process more meaningful, so that people not only have their voices heard, but actually have influence on decisions that shape air quality in their communities.

Courtesy of The Equation, UCS. By Seth D. Michaels.

#5647. Duet nabs $15M series A, plans to launch first NP-led ACO in 2027

Duet nabs $15M series A, plans to launch first NP-led ACO in 2027" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Nurse practitioner-owned primary care practice network Duet secured $15 million in series A funding to expand its clinic network and grow its partnerships and team.

Kairos led the funding round, with participation from Lerer Hippeau.

Kairos co-founder and co-CEO Alex Fiance said in a statement the company is “one of those rare businesses where everybody wins.”

“It reaches patients in need, helps clinicians become business owners, and brings costs down for the payers and the U.S. government by catching problems early,” Fiance said. “We believe Duet has the potential to reshape care across the country in a highly scalable way and are excited to help fuel their growth.”

Duet is the largest network of independent NP-owned primary care practices in the U.S., the company says. It helps practices earn compensation through its various partnerships with insurers, including Anthem, Cigna and Centene.

Duet Founder and CEO Jonathan Goldberg told Fierce Healthcare the company’s platform “combines practice advisors with AI-powered tools” to offload administrative work and give NPs more time for patient care.

“Our technology identifies people who are drifting out of care and gets them scheduled. It threads together patient data ahead of every visit, so an NP knows who is at risk and what to focus on,” Goldberg said. “And we help extend care into the home for those who need extra attention with remote technology and wraparound support.”

Goldberg continued, “There’s a lot stacked against small practices. Our mission is to help NPs show up for people that the system has overlooked.”

Patients visiting Duet clinics had an overall NPS of 92 across approximately 400,000 visits a year, Goldberg said.

“Patients often comment that this is the first time they felt heard, had someone take the time to read through their chart, or had a condition diagnosed that other clinicians had dismissed,” Goldberg said. “The clinical results follow from that. Practices that join Duet see preventive screenings for conditions like breast cancer and colon cancer rise 30%, and ER visits fall 20%. We also bring back 25% of the patients who are unengaged, and those are the patients who tend to end up worse off.”

The company has raised $22.5 million to date and works with more than 130 clinics across 21 states, executives say.

Goldberg said the company plans to use the fresh capital in three main ways: serve more clinics, deepen its value-based care partnerships with insurers and add engineers to its team. He added that Duet is launching an accelerator program in 2027 to help clinics “start strong.

Alongside the funding round, Duet announced it plans to launch the first-ever NP-led Medicare Accountable Care Organization (ACO) in 2027.

NPs will design and govern the company’s new model, which Goldberg says will allow practice owners to maintain “their autonomy while getting the technology and resources to strengthen their care.”

“NPs already practice prevention first, and this gives them a way to be recognized for it,” he said.

#5646. Prescriptions Down 25%, Overdoses Cut in Half: What Stopped a Gabapentinoid Surge?

— Study identifies three interventions that may have influenced pregabalin use

High-profile media coverage of the harms associated with the gabapentinoid pregabalin (Lyrica) and tighter safeguards coincided with fewer prescriptions and overdoses in Australia, a retrospective study showed.

Following a media campaign, tighter dispensing rules, and boxed warnings, pregabalin prescriptions and overdoses dropped significantly below projected trends -- falling by 25% and 47%, respectively -- reported Rose Cairns, PhD, of the University of Sydney, and co-authors in The BMJ.

This was a reversal from a steep upward trajectory that had persisted since 2013, the researchers noted. The shift corresponded to ongoing monthly reductions in prescriptions of 0.7% (95% CI 0.5-0.9) and in poison center overdose calls of 1.6% (95% 1.0-2.3).

Australia historically has had a big problem with pregabalin use and overdose, and many other countries face similar issues, Cairns noted.

"Importantly, the interventions that were associated with a decrease in harms included things like raising public awareness of the risks via the media and boxed warnings. These relatively 'soft' interventions can still have an impact," she told MedPage Today.

The findings are "really positive, but we think use of pregabalin is still far too high," Cairns continued. "There's a lot of off-label use for conditions with minimal evidence to back that up. And while high-risk use has decreased, we really need to continue to minimize co-use with opioids, which has been shown to increase the risk of death."

Gabapentinoids -- pregabalin and gabapentin -- were originally developed as antiseizure drugs. Subsequent indications have expanded to include neuropathic pain, postherpetic neuralgia, and fibromyalgia.

"Despite this relatively narrow range of approved indications, gabapentinoids are considered 'blockbuster' drugs," Cairns and colleagues wrote. "High-income countries have the highest rates of gabapentinoid consumption, while low- and middle-income countries have had the highest consumption growth in recent years. Most of this consumption is driven by off-label use for non-registered indications."

In the U.S., pregabalin is scheduled under the Controlled Substances Act. Gabapentin is not scheduled at the federal level but eight states have enacted legislation to classify gabapentin as a schedule V controlled substance.

In 2019, the FDA warned that respiratory depression or death may occur in patients taking pregabalin or gabapentin with opioid pain medicines or other drugs that depress the central nervous system. Despite this, co-prescribing of gabapentinoids and opioids persists.

In the U.K., one-third of substance use deaths involve a gabapentinoid, predominantly pregabalin, and deaths involving gabapentinoids have more than doubled from 2018 to 2022, Cairns and co-authors noted.

The study in Australia is "timely and rigorous, but these figures warrant caution," noted David Juurlink, MD, PhD, of the Sunnybrook Health Sciences Centre in Toronto, in an accompanying editorial. "The ecological design cannot establish causation, and poison center calls are an imperfect measure of poisoning incidence, influenced by reporting practices and patterns of healthcare use," he pointed out.

Pregabalin should be used more selectively, Juurlink argued. "Although it can provide meaningful relief in some patients with neuropathic pain or fibromyalgia, most do not achieve substantial pain relief," he wrote.

"For other painful conditions, the evidence is even less encouraging," Juurlink added. "Randomized trials and meta-analyses have found no convincing evidence of benefit for low back pain or sciatica, conditions for which the drug is nevertheless frequently prescribed. Pregabalin's commercial success, in other words, has far outstripped its demonstrated clinical utility."

To evaluate pregabalin use and misuse in Australia, Cairns and colleagues evaluated dispensing claims and poison center overdose data from 2016 to 2024, focusing on three key events: widespread media reporting about the harms of pregabalin in December 2018, tighter prescribing restrictions after April 2020, and the introduction of boxed warnings in February 2021.

The researchers randomly sampled 10% of Australians with drug subsidies and 50% of calls placed to the country's largest poison control center. During the study period, 227,864 people were dispensed 3,441,851 pregabalin prescriptions, and 4,871 pregabalin overdose calls were reported.

Use and overdoses peaked in 2018 before plateauing and declining. Dispensing dropped significantly at three time points: in December 2018 (corresponding to high-profile press coverage), in May 2020 (after tighter prescribing restrictions were introduced), and in December 2021 (after boxed warnings were in effect).

The findings are observational, the researchers acknowledged. The study analyzed subsidized prescriptions only and poisons center data may be subject to reporting bias, they added. The researchers also could not determine patients' access to pain treatments.
