#5645. Bandit Tow Companies Are Scamming People In California And Beyond

Getting in a car crash can be stressful enough, and even if no one's injured, your bank account can still feel the pain. But it's not just paying for repairs that can be expensive. The wrecker that conveniently comes along to haul your vehicle away can cost you plenty as well. In fact, that's what bandit towing companies are all about. They prey on accident victims by arriving unsolicited and using high-pressure tactics to get vulnerable drivers to agree to a tow. Next, they essentially hold those rides for ransom through outlandish fees and sky-high charges.

In one especially egregious case, Los Angeles police discovered a bandit towing company running an elaborate scam. One person would first claim to be a crash witness at the scene. The "witness" would claim to have video evidence of the accident and offer to text it to the driver involved. Once the driver passed over their digits, though, someone else would call pretending to be from their insurance company and telling them to use the scammer's truck when it showed up. It all seemed legit right up until the hapless drivers tried to get their vehicles back from those folks. In the LA example, police eventually found about 145 cars that had been illegally impounded. It was yet another case of a towing company ignoring laws meant to help people.

Nor is bandit towing limited to the West Coast. Windy City Jalopniks may be aware of Lincoln Towing Service — aka the Lincoln Park Pirates — a towing company so infamous a 1972 folk song was written about it. More recently, the National Insurance Crime Bureau (NICB) reported that predatory towing claims jumped 89% nationwide in the span from 2022 to 2024.

First off, let's be clear: It's illegal in California and other states for a tow truck to roll up to an accident scene uninvited and looking for work. In Michigan, for instance — and subject to a few disclaimers — the state's vehicle code says that "a person shall not travel to the scene of a motor vehicle accident or a disabled vehicle located on public property, property open to the public, or a state trunk line highway and solicit business for a wrecker, recovery, or towing service."

With that in mind, your obvious first step is to stay in control of the situation by only working with a towing company you've contacted yourself. As the NICB puts it, "if you didn't request the tow, say no." The major exception to this rule is if law enforcement needs your vehicle for an investigation. In that case, what happens to the vehicle is up to the authorities. You just have to hope a bad Flock doesn't lead the police to tow the wrong car. Also a good idea: Confirm that the truck that arrives is, in fact, from the service you requested.

Be sure to get all the details about the towing company you can, too, and if at all possible, do not fork over any cash upfront. The NICB notes that "legitimate towing companies" accept checks, credit cards, and other kinds of "traceable payment methods." Here you need to keep in mind that, per the Better Business Bureau, Venmo and similar digital payment services don't really qualify. In the context of consumer protection, paying with these services is basically like paying for cash. Otherwise, we could be seeing you in our next edition of Jalopnik readers' worst tow-truck experiences.

#5643. The US Navy Will Retire Its Oldest Active Aircraft Carrier After 52 Years Of Service

The oldest aircraft carrier still in service, the USS Nimitz, was commissioned on May 3, 1975, and it's quite something. Measuring approximately 1,092 feet long, it boasts a crew capacity of about 5,000, and — configuration dependent — it can carry up to 90 aircraft. It's essentially a floating air base, so rather than relying on land-based air fields, the U.S. Navy can deploy the Nimitz and operate jets, helicopters, and the like from its deck instead.

Power is sourced from a duo of Westinghouse A4W nuclear reactors, which themselves power four turbines, enabling speed of up to 30 knots. The major benefit of harnessing nuclear power on ships is that it lets them operate for decades before refueling is required. When refueling is required, though, the process takes years to carry out. A major overhaul of the Nimitz, including nuclear refueling, was finished in 2001, but the entire process took the best part of a decade, including planning.

The reason for the ship's retirement now is simple; Nimitz-class carriers were designed with a 50-year service life, and that's been reached. It should really already be tucked up in retirement, but as its replacement, the USS John F. Kennedy, has been delayed, its service has been extended to March 2027. U.S. law dictates that the Navy must keep a minimum of 11 aircraft carriers in service, so the extension of its service was necessary.

The Nimitz hasn't been the longest-serving U.S. aircraft carrier for all that long. In fact, it only took hold of that record in May 2026, pinching the title from the USS Enterprise. Other impressive numbers associated with the aircraft carrier include that in April 2023 it recorded its 350,000th arrested aircraft landing, another record, and it has also played host to more than 200,000 sailors in its time. There'is no denying, it's been a busy ship.

Now that its record-breaking run is coming to an end, questions are being raised about how it can be disassembled and recycled in the most effective, least damaging way possible. However, retirement doesn't mean immediate scrapping. It must be de-armed, safely shut down — including the defueling of its nuclear reactors — and then be prepared for dismantling. First, everything from weapons through to harmful materials, like lead and asbestos, must be carefully removed. Only then — some years down the line, when it's fully inactive and stripped — can disassembly begin.

Its replacement, the USS John F. Kennedy has been a long time coming. We watched it float for the first time seven years ago, and now we're only months away from seeing it officially enter service. The new vessel belongs to the newer Gerald R. Ford class, and it boasts some significant improvements over the Nimitz's now half-century-old design.

Traditional steam-powered catapult-style aircraft launch systems will be replaced with electromagnetic systems, although President Donald Trump has ordered later ships of the same class to revert back to steam catapults. Plus, the new ship sports far more advanced radar and combat systems. It's also far more efficient than the old design, with operations and support costs estimated at $4 billion lower over its life than the costs with Nimitz-class carriers.

The Kennedy's basic size and weight are remarkably similar its predecessor's, though, at 1,092 feet and 100,000 tons. It, too, will use nuclear power, but although its A1B reactors are smaller, they can triple the Nimitz's energy output. Ultimately, the idea is to launch a vessel that can operate aircraft more efficiently, with less maintenance, reduced operational costs, and a higher rate of sorties.

#5642. Your Holiday Gifts Don’t Have To Be Delivered By Diesel

The 2026 holiday shopping season is soon to be upon us, with gift buyers and bargain hunters perusing stores in person and online. Behind the scenes, the US freight system has been working hard to transport the goods and gifts, traveling mostly by truck as they make their way through ports, warehouses, and highways.

So, what effect will the rising price of gasoline and diesel fuel have on the year’s biggest shopping season? The historic shock in prices at the pump has driven cost increases for the goods consumers buy throughout the economy, including holiday gifts, because costs to produce and ship goods have increased. Between January and October, the average national price for a gallon of diesel rose nearly 80 percent.

But it doesn’t have to be this way. The trucks moving and delivering goods throughout the economy don’t have to be subject to the high cost and volatility of the fossil fuel market. Globally, businesses are transitioning their fleets to zero-emission trucks. In addition to their public health and environmental benefits, zero-emission trucks (and battery-electric models in particular) offer significant reductions in operating costs compared to those powered by fossil fuels. This can help protect producers and consumers from expensive and volatile fuel prices and unhealthy air.

That can help us all breathe a bit easier.

This year’s holiday shopping season is marked by several unsettling trends—record high fuel prices and the second-largest monthly jump in the Producer Price Index (PPI) since 2010, as well as the largest 12-month increase in the PPI since 2022. This jump in the PPI, the measure of the costs incurred by US businesses to make, ship, and sell their goods, was meaningfully driven by the single largest month-to-month increase in producer energy costs in the past decade and a half. Additionally, September 2026 saw the third largest increase in transportation and warehousing costs under the PPI since 2010, only behind April 2026 and March 2022—the months following the beginning of the current US-Iran conflict and Russia’s war on Ukraine, respectively.

While Santa’s reindeer are primarily fueled by carrot snacks during the holiday season, according to the Alaska Department of Fish and Game, the rest of our nation’s freight system is most often powered by dirty and increasingly expensive fossil fuels. In September, diesel fuel prices hit record highs—topping as much as $9 per gallon at some retail gas stations. Diesel-fueled trucks are the workhorse of the US freight system, carrying more freight by weight than all other transportation modes combined. Each year, tractor trucks consume over 27 billion gallons of fuel, and fuel is the second-largest cost input to trucking after labor.

Higher fuel costs can ultimately be passed along to consumers and contribute to higher price variance for goods. This is particularly the case with certain food prices. A 2023 study in Energy Economics found that fuel price shocks accounted for nearly 20 percent of the price variance of cereals. Unfortunately, this means that even the reindeer aren’t immune to fossil fuel volatility.

Consumers may never see a fuel surcharge listed on a holiday gift, but fuel costs are embedded throughout the supply chain. When diesel prices spike, it becomes more expensive to move products between ports, warehouses, stores, and homes, even for carriers that hedge fuel prices through bulk or futures fuel agreements. Powering more of those trips with comparatively stable electricity can reduce one source of sudden cost pressure before products reach shopping carts.

Battery-electric trucks can provide far greater fuel cost predictability while also offering significantly lower per-mile fuel costs than fossil-fueled trucks. Between January 2016 and September 2026 (the most recent data month at the time of this publication), annual diesel price volatility averaged 3.75 times that of commercial electricity prices, according to our analysis of Federal Reserve and Energy Information Administration data. During this time, diesel prices saw a monthly spike of greater than 10 percent seven times, including four times in 2026. Diesel prices between February and March 2026 saw the highest price spike by far at over 32 percent. By contrast, the highest spike in the national average commercial electricity price in the last decade was 4.4 percent.

While it’s true that both diesel and electricity prices have increased this year, the magnitude of those increases is hardly comparable. Between January and September, the average national price for a gallon of diesel fuel rose nearly 80 percent compared to just two percent for average commercial electricity rates.

Consumers are paying for these higher petroleum prices at the pump and checkout lines, while oil companies and shareholders are seeing a windfall: both ExxonMobil’s and Chevron’s Q3 2026 earnings were more than double those of Q3 2025.

But the costs of our fossil fuel-powered freight system extend far beyond our wallets.

Fossil-fueled medium- and heavy-duty trucks are also the nation’s largest source of smog-forming pollution and account for the largest share of the nearly 10,000 premature deaths associated with transportation pollution in the US each year, despite making up just 13 percent of the vehicles on our roads.

Trucks are a particularly significant source of pollution in areas near the warehouses and logistics centers that our holiday packages travel through. For the 66 million people living near freight corridors, warehouses, ports, and railyards nationwide, the price of a fossil fuel-powered freight system is too often paid for with their health. Recent UCS research found that the 1.7 million people living near 10 or more freight facilities face a cancer risk from air toxics that is 32 percent higher than the national average.

While freight operators cannot control geopolitics and fuel prices, they can control which fuels they choose to use. Increasingly, freight operators are considering the switch to electric trucks, which offer more stable and affordable fueling costs with far less exposure to volatile global oil markets. FedEx, for example, recently announced plans to add 2,000 electric delivery trucks to its growing zero-emission delivery fleet.

The economics for zero-emission trucks are increasingly compelling—this is partially why we’ve seen continued adoption of electric commercial vehicles despite the current federal leadership’s best efforts to prolong our reliance on fossil fuels. Even states not typically thought of as electrification front-runners have seen meaningful growth in zero-emission delivery vehicles. In 2025, over 17 percent of new commercial vans in both Mississippi and Louisiana were zero-emission vehicles (ZEVs). In Georgia, nearly 35 percent of new commercial vans registered last year were electric.

California continues to lead the nation in overall deployments of zero-emission medium-and-heavy-duty vehicles, with zero-emission models accounting for over 24 percent of all new vehicle registrations in 2025. While this is a slight backstep from 2024 levels, California continues to see growth in the deployment of zero-emission tractor trucks. In California, ZEVs comprise around 8 percent of new tractor trucks registered in 2025, and it appears the momentum is just getting started. In the first three quarters of 2026 alone, California fleets applied for state clean truck incentives for more than 1,000 new electric tractor trucks—a clear example of growing interest and demand.

Momentum toward tractor truck electrification is picking up in other parts of the country as well. In September, Catalyst Mobility, a leading clean transportation nonprofit, announced that a group of major businesses, including Microsoft and Pepsi, placed a combined order for 2,500 battery-electric Tesla Semis—the largest single order of zero-emission trucks in US history. These trucks can travel up to 500 miles on a single charge at maximum legal capacity and regain 60 percent of range in a 30-minute charge. Earlier this year, Pilot Travel Centers announced a partnership with Tesla to install high-speed chargers for electric semi-trucks across major freight corridors in California, Georgia, Nevada, New Mexico, and Texas. The first of these stations is now operational.

Despite impressive momentum, private investments and market forces alone are unlikely to reliably electrify our freight system at the needed pace. The freight industry continues to face widespread adoption barriers, including higher upfront costs for many zero-emission truck models and lack of access to widespread, reliable commercial truck charging infrastructure. At the same time, consumers have limited ability to choose shippers committed to cleaner freight. We cannot simply toggle a “green delivery” option when shopping at a local store or online.

That’s where public policy comes in: to reduce barriers to electric truck adoption and accelerate the transition toward a cleaner, more affordable freight system.

At a time when the federal government’s support for freight sustainability and modernization has reversed and is undermining previous progress, state and local governments have the opportunity and responsibility to adopt and implement policies and programs that build momentum toward an electrified freight system. Specifically, they should accelerate zero-emission truck deployment where it is technically and economically feasible today, while addressing the technological and economic barriers that limit adoption elsewhere.

Policies and programs that drive electrification today while addressing barriers could include those that:

This holiday season, millions of gifts will move through a vast network of trucks, warehouses, and distribution centers before we give them to friends and loved ones. Increasingly, those trucks don’t have to run on diesel. Electric trucks offer a path toward a freight system that produces less pollution while reducing business and consumer exposure to the volatile global fossil fuel market.

However, widescale freight electrification is unlikely to occur at the pace needed without effective public policy. Smart public policies and programs can accelerate electric truck deployment where it already makes the most economic and environmental sense, while tackling the costs and infrastructure challenges that remain.

Consumers don’t always have the option or transparency to choose cleaner freight, but we can push our elected representatives to pursue policies and programs that deliver a more sustainable way to move the economy.

If you’re an early-career scientist, seasoned scientist, or technical expert, you can join our science network for opportunities to advocate for smarter transportation policies.

Courtesy of The Equation, UCS. By Sam Wilson.

#5641. Major Change to Prostate Cancer Screening Guidelines

— ACS now recommends PSA testing every 2 years for average-risk men ages 50 to 69

Shifting gears on prostate cancer screening, the American Cancer Society (ACS) now recommends screening with prostate-specific antigen (PSA) every 2 years for average-risk men ages 50 to 69.

Since the release of its previous guideline 16 years ago, the ACS had recommended that average-risk men consult with a physician before deciding for themselves whether to undergo a screening PSA test.

The new ACS recommendations also strengthened guidelines for men at high risk for prostate cancer, recommending annual screening beginning at age 45 for Black men and those with a strong family history of the disease. The organization previously recommended that those men discuss the pros and cons of screening with a physician at age 40 or 45.

The changes reflect the accumulation of stronger evidence that screening PSA tests reduce prostate cancer mortality and diagnosis of late-stage disease, according to the updated guidelines from Robert Smith, PhD, ACS senior vice president for early cancer detection science, and colleagues, appearing in CA: A Cancer Journal for Clinicians.

"We've just reached a point in our understanding of prostate cancer, especially that [screening] can reduce the risk of being diagnosed with metastatic disease and dying of prostate cancer," Smith told MedPage Today. "We have good protocols for avoiding harms."

Smith noted that the U.S. Preventive Services Task Force (USPSTF) has not updated their prostate cancer screening guidelines since 2018. For average-risk men, the USPSTF recommends discussion-backed decisions on PSA testing for men ages 55 to 69.

"I cannot tell you what [their recommendations] would be if they updated them today, but they did not have the data in 2018 that we had in 2025 with the update of the European trial data showing the endurance of the mortality reduction," said Smith.

The update aligns with recent evidence supporting PSA screening for prostate cancer as "one of the most important steps men can take to protect their health," said Ash Tewari, MD, of the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai in New York City.

"We have strong evidence that PSA testing can reduce the risk of dying from prostate cancer, and advances in MRI and other diagnostic tools are helping us identify potentially dangerous cancers while avoiding unnecessary procedures," he said. "Knowing your risk, getting screened regularly, and having these conversations early can save lives."

With the update, the ACS takes a clear position on prostate cancer screening, said Mark Ashamalla, MD, of Episcopal Health Services in New York City.

"For years, the ACS largely focused on encouraging men to discuss the pros and cons of PSA screening with their doctors," said Ashamalla. "Now they're taking a much clearer position and actually recommending regular screening. For men at average risk, that means a PSA blood test every 2 years between ages 50 and 69, while Black men and those with a strong family history should begin annual screening at age 45. This is a significant shift and reflects growing evidence that regular screening can reduce the risk of advanced prostate cancer and save lives."

"The goal isn't to find and treat every prostate cancer," he added. "It's to catch the dangerous ones early enough to make a difference. I think that's the biggest takeaway from these new guidelines."

By way of the guidelines, the ACS also recommends:

The updated guidelines are supportive of the recent trend toward active surveillance as the primary option for men with low-risk prostate cancer.

"It's important to recognize that prostate cancer screening has evolved over the past 30 years," said Smith. "More and more people appreciate that very worrisome harms associated with therapy can be avoided, especially if a man is diagnosed with prostate cancer that is not aggressive. Active treatment should be avoided until such time as it appears that the disease has become aggressive. Some men may be diagnosed with a prostate cancer that's not very aggressive, and they will live with it in that state for the rest of their lives. [They can] avoid harms that are quite diminishing to quality of life."

"On the other hand, if a man's prostate cancer becomes more aggressive, and treatment is necessary, then the process of active surveillance will at least have postponed it for a number of years, allowing men to not have to live with those [treatment-related] side effects," said Smith.

#5640. How To Get A Screaming Deal On Your Next Car Rental

Whether you're flying with the family for a week of sun and sand with a whole lot of luggage and need transportation, or headed home for the holidays via I-94 and dread the overflow of humans and gifts and luggage all crammed into your daily driver, you may find yourself in need of a rental car.

But won't that be expensive? Perhaps not. We researched ways to save money on rental cars and interviewed two travel experts to get the inside scoop on keeping costs down. Victoria Fricke, owner of luxury travel agency Vic's Vacations, voted Indianapolis' Best Travel Agency, and Sharky Laguana, CEO of Bandago, which specializes in van rentals for bands and film crews, both lent their expertise. Laguana also chairs the board of the American Rental Car Association.

With our eye on reducing costs, ur recommendations include a range from planning ahead and shopping around to making deliberate decisions about insurance coverage when picking up the rental.

Laguana's recommends you book your car as soon as you know your travel dates. "If you book well in advance you can often find deals that don't exist days before pickup. The reason for this is straightforward. Car rental companies rely on early bookings to gauge demand, and having confirmed bookings in their back pocket allows them more room to experiment with higher prices as the date draws near."

Just be aware that if you do pre-pay, you may not be able to get a full refund if your plans change. For instance, Hertz will charge $100 if you cancel more than 24 hours prior to the reservation start date, and $200 if you're within 24 hours. Alamo is more generous, tagging you with a $50 fee for canceling before the 24-hour window and $100 within 24 hours of the loan start date.

As for weighing renting in town or at the airport, most of the time, according to reporting by NerdWallet, off-airport rental car reservations are cheaper. They looked at 20 different destinations and found that for rental car reservations lasting seven days, the average savings was $126 versus renting at the airport.

Still, it's a good idea to look at each location (city versus airport) and study the actual rates. You might also find a more limited selection of cars in cities than at the airport. And definitely read the fine print if you're thinking about a city pickup and airport drop-off, because that can add costly one-way fees.

Laguana explains that often, "Major online-travel aggregators (Expedia, Priceline, Kayak, etc.) have clauses in their agreements with car rental companies that prevent the rental companies from advertising rates lower than what the aggregators offer." Those deals don't necessarily cause higher prices for consumers. "Using a broker doesn't automatically mean you are paying more. But many companies, particularly the smaller independent companies, don't list their cars on aggregators." Fricke recommends online travel aggregators too, explaining, "I always start on Kayak. It's a great resource to pull all brands in comparison. I find this helps when searching for certain vehicle types like large sprinter vans. From there, I use that pricing once I've found my best-priced option, and I go direct." Meaning, after she finds the lowest price, she books directly through the car rental company.

While aggregators are a great place to start, you might miss a better deal if you only shop through those channels. Laguana explains, "Sometimes a simple Google search or chat with an AI agent will turn up rates significantly lower than what the aggregators have listed."

As for which rental car agencies are cheapest, NerdWallet's study of rental car companies found that Budget, Hertz, and Dollar consistently loaned cars for less. It's actually odd that there's any rate competition at all, since all of the major rental car brands are owned by just three companies.  Still, since you will be cross-shopping, know that Enterprise averaged $480 for seven days of rental, which was way cheaper than the $680 that National charged. Luckily, some of these players, such as Hertz, also have solid ratings and rent cool cars.

If you're a AAA member, you can save up to 20% off the base rate of all rental cars from Hertz, Dollar, and Thrifty. You can also add an extra driver for free. Costco pre-negotiates cheaper rates for its members and lets you add a second driver at no extra cost, which rental car agencies definitely charge more for.

Frequent-flyer programs also offer discounts on rental cars. For instance, United Airlines frequent flyers save up to 35% on base rates with Avis and Budget. USAA members (available to active duty military, veterans, and their families) can get up to 35% off current rental rates from Avis, Budget, Enterprise, Hertz, National, and Alamo rental agencies.‍ ‍

It's also possible your company or university alumni affiliation may offer discount codes. It's worth asking your employer or checking an alumni website to see if these exist – and you may have to present proof of the affiliation when you fetch the car. Also, make sure these codes aren't restricted to corporate travel rather than travel for pleasure, since violating those policies might endanger not just your affiliation but coverage during the rental.

"When I rent cars while traveling, I usually get most of the insurance products (although I will usually pass on the personal effects coverage)," Laguana says. "My reasoning: I'm driving in a location I'm not super familiar with, in a car I'm not super familiar with. Paying a little extra to avoid having to file a claim with my own insurance company makes sense to me." And for many, that peace of mind may well be worth the cost.

But if you have comprehensive car insurance, you might already have car rental coverage. You'll still be on the hook for your deductible, which you would be in an accident with your own car, too.

Also, check with your credit card company on their policies for car rentals. Often they bake in extra insurance coverage as long as you charge the car to them. For instance, the Chase Sapphire Reserve card adds $75,000 of damage coverage to the rental car. But that only applies to the car you're renting, not potential damage you might cause to another car or people outside the rental.

The key here is that the credit card saves you from taking out additional insurance from the car rental company. In fact, Chase's policy says you must decline the rental car agency's collision damage waiver or loss damage waiver (CDW and LDW) insurance so that Chase is your backstop. But, again, you'll still need your own car insurance supporting you in the event of an accident that causes other damage. This is often called supplemental liability protection, and you need to be certain your car insurance includes this. FYI, if you're traveling abroad, the rental company might say you have to buy that through them regardless.

Some rental car agencies offer a "mystery rental" option, meaning you don't get to choose the type or size of the car. "The mystery rental is usually a car that is not selling well," Laguana explains. "But it can still be a great deal, because the car rental company won't want to list that vehicle at such a low rate and have it find its way into various websites and search engines. So if you don't have specific needs and don't care what kind of car you get, this might be the best option."

As for other hacks and cautions, Laguana says renters can get into trouble by, "Not examining all the terms and conditions. Not long ago, comparing deals took a lot of work; now, AI makes it much easier. If you have a brand you trust, go with that brand. But if you don't know where to start, using AI can help you compare your options." That can help you avoid some of the most common rental car tourist scams.

Laguana goes further, explaining that there can be all sorts of hidden fees for non-vacation car rentals, too. "In our little sector, the passenger van rental business, we compete with businesses that have wildly different business models. Some offer cheap rates but then add required cleaning fees, additional driver fees, higher priced insurance products or lower mileage limits to make up the difference."

To avoid a scratch or ding getting chalked up to you during your rental period, Fricke advises clients "To ensure you take a video walking around the vehicle, checking for any damage (don't forget the inside). This ensures if there's any existing damage the rental car company doesn't place blame on you." If your inspection turns up damage that's not listed by the rental agency you need to call that out so they mark that down before you begin your loan.

Another good idea is to use an app like Gas Buddy so you know the price of gas near the rental agency. Most companies want to charge you their own per-gallon toll (usually higher than the going rate) if you bring the car back empty. Otherwise you need to fill it up before giving the car back. Maybe it's worth avoiding visiting a gas station before you return the car, but check the average gas price versus what they're charging so you're aware of the difference.

Finally, while you may want to rent someone's cool car through a peer-to-peer app like Turo, some policies, for instance getting coverage through your credit card, could be excluded. Chase explicitly excludes such rentals. Turo includes liability insurance in their loans (which covers third parties and their property), but says you should check with your car insurer to see if it covers peer-to-peer car loans — and cautions that most don't. For that reason they offer several tiers of insurance to cover damage to the loaned car. However, only their Premier tier prevents you from owing money for damages.

#5639. Brigham and Women's avoids 4,000-nurse strike with 11th hour deal

Brigham and Women's avoids 4,000-nurse strike with 11th hour deal" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Massachusetts Nurses Association and Mass General Brigham announced late Wednesday that their negotiating teams have reached a tentative deal, averting, by just hours, a potentially historic strike by 4,000 Brigham and Women's Hospital nurses.

The open-ended demonstration was scheduled to begin Thursday morning, and had previously been postponed to give union members time to vote on a prior "last, best offer" from the health system, which was rejected. Their planned strike would have been the largest nurses strike in Massachusetts' history.

“I’m incredibly proud of the Brigham nurses who stood strong to protect the world-class patient care we provide,” Kelly Morgan, a labor and delivery nurse and chair of the BWH MNA Bargaining Committee. “We secured this agreement with the help of countless community members, patients, unions and elected officials who supported us for many months. By standing together, we protected patients and the future of union nursing care at the Brigham.”

The bargaining committee said it would recommend members vote to ratify the agreement, which they said addresses "the hospital's reliance" on staffing contingencies like temporary nurses and float nurse positions, maintains "affordable" health insurance benefits and new steps at the top of the hospital's wage scale for experienced nurses. Specific highlights on these and other discussion points are outlined in the union's announcement.

Brigham and Women's Hospital said it was pleased with the tentative deal, which it noted was "within the financial limits" of the prior deal it had presented last month.

"We deeply value our Brigham nurses and believe this agreement recognizes their important contributions while supporting the long-term sustainability of our hospital and our ability to care for our patients and communities," the hospital said in a statement. "We are also grateful to the nursing leaders, clinicians and staff across Mass General Brigham who worked tirelessly to ensure we were prepared to provide safe, high-quality care under any circumstance.”

In prior statements, the hospital had said it had been onboarding at least 800 agency nurses to maintain operations, with minimal adjustments to operating hours at some ambulatory sites. The final sticking points in this week's marathon bargaining sessions, per the hospital, were not related to patient or workplace safety issues, but to areas of compensation like wages and benefits.

Of note, the negotiations landed amid an announcement from parent system Mass General Brigham that a $300 million budget shortfall had triggered a new round of job cuts affecting both clinical and operational programs, including the organization's hospital-at-home program.

Union nurses at Brigham and Women's Hospital are set to strike in the coming weeks after failed contract negotiations, in what could be the largest open-ended nurse strike in Massachusetts history.

Brigham and Women's Hospital said in a statement it received notice from the Massachusetts Nurses Association (MNA) on Nov. 1 about an open-ended strike beginning Thursday, Nov. 12 at 7 a.m.

"While we had hoped to avoid another strike, we are prepared to continue providing safe, high-quality care throughout any work stoppage, building on our experience and extensive contingency planning," the statement read. "We deeply value and respect our Brigham nurses and remain committed to reaching a fair and responsible agreement. Our latest offer provides market-leading compensation and competitive benefits while recognizing our responsibility to our patients, our entire workforce and the long-term financial sustainability of Brigham and Women’s Hospital."

Negotiations with MNA are set to resume Nov. 9, the hospital said.

MNA said in a statement negotiations will focus on "securing a fair contract that meets the needs of the nurses, their patients and the community members they serve." Nurses are seeking the following terms as part of a fair contract, per the union:

"We are ready to strike but are equally prepared to negotiate a fair contract that protects patient care, respects nurses, and averts the strike," said Kelly Morgan, chair of the union's bargaining committee for the hospital. “Brigham nurses are resolved to fight for as long as it takes for patient safety and the professional respect we deserve.”

Union nurses at Brigham and Women's Hospital voted to reject management's "last, best and final offer" on updated contracts, potentially steering the dispute back toward what could be the largest open-ended nurse strike in Massachusetts history.

The Massachusetts Nurses Association had previously scheduled the strike for Oct. 14, but postponed to give its members a chance to vote on the proposal from Mass General Brigham, the hospital's parent system. The union said it has requested to resume contract negotiations this week, with the hospital responding in its own public statement this it "is prepared to maintain uninterrupted care and respond to any steps the union may take."

MGB's rejected offer would have retained the hospital's existing nursing wage progression system of annual 5% step increases until 20 years tenure, with the addition of two additional 2.5% increases at the top of the prior structure. The union has said it is seeking limits on the use of temporary nurses, more affordable health insurance benefits and “a meaningful cost-of-living wage increase” that includes a differential for nurses floating between units.

“This vote shows the resolve of Brigham nurses, who are fighting for patient safety and for the professional respect we deserve," Kelly Morgan, chair of the union's bargaining committee for the hospital, said in a weekend release. "It’s [time for] MGB to return to the table ready to negotiate in good faith to reach a fair agreement that recognizes the value of Brigham nurses and the expertise they offer for the care of some of the region’s most complex patients every day.”

Brigham and Women's Hospital said it was "disappointed" by the rejection, and that its proposal "reflected the hospital’s commitment to investing in our nurses while supporting long-term sustainability, including meaningful compensation and benefit enhancements that kept Brigham nursing pay among the highest in the market."

Notably, the announcement of the member vote's outcome coincided with news that MGB has begun a new round of layoffs affecting both clinical and operational programs. The unspecified number of cuts reflect a financial need to further adjust operating MGB's structure even after roughly 1,500 people were laid off in 2024, the system said.

Nurses at Brigham and Women’s Hospital have lifted their notice of an Oct. 14 strike after their union and Mass General Brigham management made progress toward a potential contract deal late Saturday night, the sides said in statements.

The 4,000-member open-ended strike would be the largest in state history if launched, and follows months of contract negotiations between the health system and the Massachusetts Nurses Association (see that coverage below).

A formal deal has yet to be struck, though the union said it agreed to call off its 10-day strike notice "given the complexity of the agreement and the impact of a potential strike on our nurses and the community." The proposal is being presented to members for a vote, which might yield a new 10-day notice if rejected.

Brigham and Women's Hospital, in a Sunday statement, said its "last, best and final offer ... reflects significant movement on several of the union's priorities while maintaining a responsible approach to supporting nurses, patients and the long-term strength of the hospital."

It added that the offer retains the hospital's existing nursing wage progression system of annual 5% step increases until 20 years tenure, with the addition of two additional 2.5% increases at the top of the prior structure. The withdrawn notice also saved the hospital from having to implement its contingency staffing plan of hiring temporary nurses.

"We are thankful the MNA has pulled its indefinite strike notice and will allow its members to vote on the hospital's final offer," the hospital said. The union's statement did not specify the timing of that vote.

Editor's note: On Oct. 2, the Massachusetts Nurses Association announced an Oct. 14, 7:00 a.m. start time for their strike if no deal is reached.

Nurses at Brigham and Women’s Hospital have voted to authorize an open-ended, 4,000-member strike that could become the largest nurses’ strike in Massachusetts history.

The Massachusetts Nurses Association announced the 93% in favor vote that was held Thursday evening. No date has been set yet by the union, which is legally required to give employer Mass General Brigham (MGB) 10 days' notice.

“Brigham nurses have made clear that we are prepared to stay on strike for as long as it takes to win a contract that protects our patients and respects the nurses who care for them,” Kelly Morgan, a labor and delivery nurse and chair of the Brigham and Women’s Hospital MNA Bargaining Committee, said in the union’s announcement. “An open-ended strike is a serious step that will undoubtedly create a healthcare crisis in Massachusetts. MGB can prevent the strike by returning to the table and negotiating the contract our nurses need to keep providing world-class care.”

Contract negotiations between the sides have been ongoing since last November, with the prior agreement expiring on March 31. In July, the nurses (plus an additional 450 home care clinicians) conducted a one-day awareness strike that was also a record breaker for the state and the first at Brigham and Women’s, a flagship hospital and founding member of the nonprofit system.

Demonstrations have continued in the weeks since, including a rally held earlier this week outside of an MGB-hosted World Medical Innovation Forum, where bargaining members painted a contrast between executives’ conversations with investors on AI and the demands of their clinical workforce.

The hospital, in statements given to press, said “we remain committed to bargaining in good faith and have put forward a fair and responsible proposal that maintains Brigham nurses’ market-leading compensation, preserves meaningful wage growth and competitive benefits, and balances those investments with the long-term financial sustainability of our system,” the hospital said in a statement given to press.

The hospital has previously said it has found agreements with the union on several issues. However, the union has previously said that talks stalled in mid-August and reiterated Thursday that it still is seeking limits on the use of temporary nurses, more affordable health insurance benefits and “a meaningful cost-of-living wage increase” that includes a differential for nurses floating between units.

MGB is the largest private employer in Massachusetts with roughly 85,000 workers and $22.8 billion in annual revenue, making it among the country’s largest nonprofit health systems. Financial results released last month outlined a $15 million operating income (0.1% operating margin) across nine months of its ongoing fiscal year, up from a $44 million operating loss (-0.3% operating margin), though its nine-month bottom line including nonoperating gains like investment returns was $1.9 billion. Early last year saw the organization undertake the largest layoffs in its history, when it cut about 1,500 workers.

The union has pointed to MGB’s bottom line (nearly $2.4 billion across fiscal 2025), substantial total assets ($35.8 billion) and the compensation of executives (almost $9.2 million for CEO Anne Klibanski, M.D.) in its calls for stronger contracts.

#5638. Hydropower Generation in the Southwest Fell to Record Lows This Summer

Prolonged drought conditions affecting the Colorado River Basin led to record low hydropower generation this summer in the Southwestern electricity supply region, which includes Arizona, New Mexico, and small parts of Nevada and California. Hydropower generation from April through August 2026 was 19% lower than during the same period last year and 28% lower than the 10-year average, according to the latest data from our Form EIA-923 survey.

Water elevations at Lake Powell and Lake Mead, which are formed by the Glen Canyon Dam and Hoover Dam, respectively, hit record lows. To be able to generate hydropower at the Glen Canyon Dam, Lake Powell should have an elevation of at least 3,490 feet, also known as the minimum power pool. At the end of September, this elevation reached 3,518 feet, less than 1% above minimum power pool. The Hoover Dam requires an elevation of at least 950 feet at Lake Mead, which by the end of September reached 1,038 feet, 9% higher than minimum power pool. The U.S. Bureau of Reclamation manages most dams across the Colorado River Basin. Seven states (Arizona, California, Colorado, Nevada, New Mexico, Utah, and Wyoming) depend on the river for hydropower generation and for other water supply needs. To mitigate the risk of Lake Powell’s water elevation falling below the minimum power pool, water was released from Flaming Gorge, a reservoir located in the Upper Colorado River Basin. Additionally, water releases from Lake Powell were reduced to maintain an elevation of at least 3,510 feet given the most probable water inflows.

However, lower water releases from Lake Powell could also impact water elevation downstream at Lake Mead, in turn affecting hydropower generation at the Hoover Dam. To address this possibility, the Department of the Interior decided to reduce the collective water consumption of California, Arizona, and Nevada by 1.25 million acre-feet each year over the next two years.

We expect hydropower generation in the Southwest will total 6.13 billion kilowatthours (BkWh) in 2026 in our November Short-Term Energy Outlook (STEO), 9% lower than last year and 24% lower than the 10-year average. In 2027, we expect hydropower generation in the Southwest to total 6.1 BkWh, 0.5% lower than this year. This year’s El Niño could bring more precipitation than normal, providing some drought relief and improving the hydropower generation forecast in the region. However, one good water year likely won’t eliminate the effects of multiple years of water scarcity.

According to the STEO, hydropower generation in the Southwest is expected to account for about 4% of the region’s generation mix in 2026. That share has declined from a peak of 8% in 2011.

Article from Today in Energy. Principal contributor: Lindsay Aramayo.

The EIA collects, analyzes, and disseminates independent and impartial energy information to promote sound policymaking, efficient markets, and public understanding of energy and its interaction with the economy and the environment.

#5637. Trump's FDA Pick on Thin Ice; AI to Fight Insurance Denials; Coffee and Gut Health

— Health news and commentary gathered by MedPage Today staff

#5636. If The Temerario's Exhaust Note Stopped You Buying One, Lamborghini Has Some Good News Made From Titanium

Despite the 10,000-rpm redline and its status as a mid-engined Italian supercar, the Lamborghini Temerario makes a noise that isn't universally loved. Our own Daniel Golson says this car's twin-turbo V8 is one of the best engines he's ever experienced, and Car Throttle says it sounds "incredible." Our own Andy Kalmowitz says it sounds "broken" below 3,000 rpm, while the Throttle House/Grand Tour guys once remarked that unless you're knocking on the redline's door, the Lambo simply "doesn't sound very good."

I think the Temerario having to live in the shadow of its naturally aspirated V10 Huracán predecessor has a lot to do with this mixed reception, but either way, Lamborghini has seemingly heard the feedback and is now raising the supercar's aural ceiling by offering a titanium exhaust from Akrapovič as a factory option. The upgrade is very much positioned as a sound enhancement, not necessarily a performance one, because in the first line of its press release, Lambo says it was "developed to amplify the natural acoustic character of its V8 engine while utilizing lightweight engineering."

These new optional pipes feature a different geometry and layout, physically wider tubes, custom manifolds, and a tailored silencer architecture. Strategic insulation keeps things cool in hot zones, and NVH was carefully calibrated so specific frequencies are enhanced. Lambo says this gives the Temerario "deep notes" at low rpm and an "incisive acoustic signature" at high ones.

It's made of high-grade titanium alloys, uses precision-cast valve housings, and is hand-assembled "across every component." Lamborghini says it weighs 25.1 pounds, 12% less than the standard exhaust, which means it cuts about 3.4 pounds from the car's curb weight. The tip is black to blend in with the car's rear end, and of course it says Akrapovič on the top so everyone at Cars & Coffee can know you spent the extra dough even when the car is off. There's good news for people who already have Temerarios looking for some extra, better decibels: the part will be available as a retrofit package.

We should note that, before this, the Temerario could already be had with a titanium muffler if you went for the lightweight Alleggerita Package that also bundled in a bunch of carbon-fiber bits, but this Akrapovič system is said to be new and therefore definitely not the same thing. Akrapovič is a Slovenian company that specializes in aftermarket exhausts, and has arguably become the name in the space, a bit like how Brembo is with brakes or Recaro is with seats. Lambo previously worked with Akrapovič on upgraded exhausts for the Urus SUV and Huracán STO.

For reference, the Temerario uses a twin-turbo 4.0-liter V8 along with three electric hybrid motors to produce 907 horsepower. For those who were on the fence about buying one because it apparently doesn't sound quite as nutty as that old V10 (I mean, that's why I haven't pulled the trigger yet), perhaps these new Slovenian pipes will change things for you.

#5635. ONC, CMS move electronic prior authorization into real-world testing

ONC, CMS move electronic prior authorization into real-world testing" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Office of the National Coordinator for Health IT (ONC) is launching a voluntary industry testing initiative to help healthcare organizations prepare for federal electronic prior authorization requirements taking effect in 2027.

ONC is collaborating with the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services (CMS) to launch a series of industry testing events in production-like environments to advance end-to-end electronic prior authorization (ePA), the agencies announced Thursday.

A federal rule requires some payers to implement an application programming interface (API) to support electronic prior authorization processes by January 1. Impacted payers include Medicare Advantage, Medicaid managed care, CHIP and ACA exchange plans.

The focused, 10- to 12-week testing sprints will bring together payers, providers, hospitals, electronic health record (EHR) developers, intermediaries and other industry partners to test their own workflows across connected systems and identify gaps before broader implementation, CMS and ONC officials said.

"Federal regulations and industry standards have laid important groundwork for ePA, but our work is not done," Tom Keane, M.D., National Coordinator for Health IT, said in a statement. "With our colleagues at CMS, we are bringing partners together to advance the next phase of implementation testing to accelerate ePA progress in the near term."

Organizations interested in participating can learn more and sign up for the initiative by November 20, according to ONC. Participant feedback and lessons learned will inform future testing and implementation activities.

"Regulations and industry standards have laid important groundwork for ePA, but real work remains to put this policy into practice. These events mark the next phase of implementation testing, bringing trading partners together to examine end-to-end workflows in production-like environments," ONC leaders Ashley Hain and Molly Prieto, principal deputy director of the Office of Standards, Certification and Analysis, wrote in a blog post.

During the testing sprints, participants will use their own workflows to examine how prior authorization requests and decisions move across connected systems. "The focus is on finding technical barriers and workflow gaps that could arise in real-world implementation, while organizations still have an opportunity to address them before broader deployment," Hain and Prieto wrote.

ONC and CMS will use the findings from the testing events to make practical improvements. "Some findings may point to standards that need clarification or revision. Others may reveal a need for clearer educational materials for developers, providers and hospitals, or more useful implementer guidance," Hain and Prieto wrote.

"We aim to identify critical updates quickly and help industry prioritize the improvements needed for successful implementation across care settings. The purpose is to turn the progress already made through regulations and standards into workflows that reduce administrative burden and support timely care," the ONC leaders wrote.

The testing events will include workflows and real-world scenarios using the HL7 FHIR Da Vinci implementation guides for Coverage Requirements Discovery (CRD), Documentation Templates and Rules (DTR), and Prior Authorization Support (PAS). Each event will include coordinated scenarios, based on high-volume clinical services that require prior authorization and supporting resources to help participants test together, ONC said.

ONC and CMS are working with the MITRE-led Health Federally Funded Research and Development Center and Drummond Group to support stakeholder recruitment, readiness discussions, participant matching and testing coordination. Drummond Group will operate FHIRplace, the virtual environment where developers, providers, payers and intermediaries will test with their trading partners.

CMS and ONC encouraged participation from organizations of all sizes, including smaller organizations and those serving rural communities.

#5634. 10 Reasons for the Big Blue Wave

I wrote the other day about a top reason US voters said they voted for Democrats in such large numbers — corruption in the Trump household, Trump administration, and Republican Party. It was surprising to me that this showed up as the biggest factor for so many people … even though it was also a huge one for me. However, I think everyone knows what the most significant reason was for the dramatic, historic shift in voting. It’s the economy, stupid! That’s the famous old quote from James Carville, a political strategist working for Bill Clinton when he was running for president in 1992, and it’s as relevant as ever. With that in mind, let’s take a look at some of the top reasons for the Big Blue Wave.

Inflation has hit and hit and hit. Donald Trump said while campaigning for president in 2024, “Starting on day one, we will end inflation and make America affordable again.” However, of course, beyond the fact that he didn’t come close to doing that and was just making an obviously false promise, Trump actually jacked up inflation significantly with his obsessive, high tariffs and with his war on Iran. Whereas it’s often hard to tie inflation to a specific presidential policy, in this case, we had two specific presidential policies that clearly caused inflation! Even when there are no clear links, voters blame the president for such matters, so imagine how much they must have done so in this case!

Aside from inflation, the economy overall isn’t great. The country isn’t adding a bunch of jobs, wages and salaries aren’t rising much, and people by and large don’t feel optimistic about their financial futures. So, combined with quickly rising costs, a very meh economy had voters wanting change.

As already discussed at length, voters were fed up with corruption in the Republican Party. As some CleanTechnica readers pointed out, if people were prospering financially and not being hit with severe inflation, many of them probably wouldn’t care about the corruption. However, when considering #1 and #2, people naturally get quite frustrated with blatant corruption and essentially high-volume robbery from Donald Trump, the Trump family, Trump staffers, and the Republican Party broadly.

Naturally, this is heavily linked to #1, since it is largely this war that has led to gas prices rising so much. However, also more broadly, Americans are fed up with forever wars in the Middle East, there’s never been a good and strong case for why Trump started this war (as tends to be the case with these wars in the Middle East), and many people really don’t like seeing innocent schoolchildren being bombed to death. Trump was supposed to be an anti-war president (I know that many people believed that, even though Trump is a naturally aggressive, violent person who seemed to me like someone who would obviously get into wars). Voters who cared a lot about this topic might have felt betrayed by Trump and swung strongly away from him and the Republican Party (which has a tendency to get us into these oil wars in the Middle East).

Even for many people who thought we needed stronger actions to stop people from coming to the United States, the inhumane, deadly, Gestapo-like ICE army — often including violent people with their faces covered and without ID acting like the mafia — was a step or ten too far. There are countless stories of good, innocent people being jailed without cause, or even murdered, by this Nazi-like police force. There was clearly a huge blue shift in certain places as a result of this.

Many voters have gotten extremely concerned about Republicans under Trump’s leadership eroding the foundations of our democracy. While this became a major problem in Trump’s first term, he has gone much further in his second term. Apparently, more people have woken up to this problem and it has freaked them out.

A massive rollout of AI and data centers — often feeling completely forced on us — has upset a lot of Americans. This does come back to #1, too. Giant data centers have jacked up electricity prices a ton in several regions. Others are threatened with the same thing happening. The same thing has also happened from the crypto craze, which more and more people have also found to be a scam. Seeing billions upon billions trillions of dollars funneled toward AI companies while people are struggling, and then seeing electricity prices rising significantly — surprise, surprise — pisses people off and makes them vote for change.

No matter how you cut it, people vote based on money — in particular, their own money. Whether it’s food prices, gas prices, electricity prices, clothing prices, doll prices, computer prices, car prices, or other prices, people don’t like it when they have to pay a lot more money for stuff — especially when they aren’t making much more money, or more money at all. Simply put, this matter deserves extra weight, because the critical thing swing voters are often voting on is just that — money.

Of course, there are solar, wind, and EV aspects to all of this. Trump has made the US spend a fortune to stop wind and solar farms from providing the US with cheap electricity, and he has made the US spend a fortune keeping old, dirty coal power plants open. High gas prices have also pushed people to buy electric cars, but mostly in other countries where there’s more EV choice and more people have more sense on this topic. However, by and large, I don’t think American voters really shifted much based on Trump stopping renewable energy projects, promoting coal and gas, or limiting EV choice in the United States. People voting for Democrats on climate and cleantech matters were already voting for Democrats based on these issues. It’s unlikely many people at all shifted from red to blue because of these factors.

Zach is tryin' to help society help itself one word at a time. He spends most of his time here on CleanTechnica as its editor-in-chief and CEO. Zach is recognized globally as an electric vehicle, solar energy, and energy storage expert. He has presented about electric vehicles and renewable energy at conferences in India, the UAE, Ukraine, Poland, Germany, the Netherlands, the USA, Canada, and Curaçao.

#5633. U.S. on the Verge of Forfeiting Major Public Health Victory on Measles

— The nation is having its worst measles year in decades

International health experts are meeting this week to determine if the U.S. and Mexico have forfeited one of their greatest public health victories -- the elimination of measles.

Measles is one of the most contagious diseases known to medicine, but vaccines had safely stopped the virus' spread in the U.S. for 30 years. Over those decades, measles had become a medical rarity in the U.S., with deaths from the disease virtually unheard of. But in the past 2 years, large outbreaks have hit multiple states, spawning thousands of illnesses, including about a dozen deaths.

Experts expect that the meeting, convened by the Pan American Health Organization, or PAHO, will show that measles has officially returned to the U.S. and Mexico -- that the virus is now continually spreading within the nations' borders.

"It's a big deal. The decision represents us moving backwards," said David Margolius, MD, director of Cleveland's health department and a leader in a coalition of big U.S. city health departments.

Here's a look at the meeting's deliberations and why it's important.

International Health Experts Are Poring Over Measles Data This Week

In a closed-door meeting this week, a PAHO advisory commission is looking at genetic evidence to determine if the same measles virus has been spreading for 1 year or more in the outbreaks that have torn through North America starting in 2024.

"It's solely based on genetics," said Amesh Adalja, MD, an infectious disease doctor in the Pittsburgh area and a senior scholar at the Johns Hopkins Center for Health Security.

If so, the commission will say the country can no longer be considered to have "eliminated" measles, meaning a return to sustained circulation of the virus. Last year, PAHO stripped Canada of the measles-free designation it had had since 1998.

The commission will present its determination to PAHO Director Jarbas Barbosa, MD, MPH, PhD, a former Brazilian health official, who makes the final determination. An announcement of the findings is being scheduled for next week, PAHO officials said.

The CDC Says Loss of Measles Elimination Status Won't Change Its Approach

A PAHO decision to strip the U.S. of its measles elimination status will not change how federal health officials are addressing outbreaks, CDC Director Erica Schwartz, MD, MPH, said this week.

"The measles elimination designation is not what protects Americans. Vaccination does. We know where transmission is occurring and we're working with state and local health departments to reach those communities. Losing a designation doesn't mean we abandon an evidence-based approach. It means we remain focused on stopping outbreaks," she said in the statement.

But many public health experts say such an occasion demands a stepped-up, full-throated campaign to promote vaccinations and provide more advice to doctors. They fault what they say is a politically fraught, muted federal response, and blame U.S. Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., who was a leading voice in the anti-vaccine movement before President Donald Trump put him in charge of the nation's health agencies.

Kennedy has moved to downsize the childhood vaccination schedule and reshape a key vaccine advisory board, and has lauded Trump's executive order calling for spacing out the measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine. Such a move runs counter to medical groups' recommendations, and likely would result in fewer children getting fully vaccinated, experts say.

The U.S. Is Having Its Worst Measles Year in Decades

Measles typically begins with a high fever followed by a telltale rash that starts on the face and neck. Most people recover, but it's one of the leading causes of death among young children, according to the World Health Organization. Serious complications, including blindness and swelling of the brain, are more common in young children and adults older than 30.

The U.S. achieved measles elimination status in 2000, after decades of vaccination campaigns brought the childhood measles vaccination rate to above 97%. Health experts say a 95% vaccination rate is needed so that when an infected traveler enters a community, so many people are vaccinated that it will not spark an outbreak.

But U.S. vaccination rates have been slipping for years, in part because of disproven claims that the vaccine can cause autism. Trump claimed earlier this year, without evidence, that the MMR vaccine -- which is credited with saving millions of lives globally -- is possibly "quite lethal." Last year, the childhood vaccination rate fell to about 92% nationally, and was much lower in certain states and certain communities.

Measles exploded last year in large outbreaks in Texas, Arizona, Utah, and South Carolina. And the more than 5,100 confirmed cases this year are already double the 2025 tally, driven largely by an outbreak in Pennsylvania that has dwarfed the earlier ones. The U.S. has now seen eight measles-associated deaths this year -- more than any year in more than three decades -- and is enduring its worst year for measles illnesses since 1991.

At Least 50 People Have Died From Measles in Mexico

A measles vaccine did not become available in Mexico until years after a measles vaccine was first licensed -- in 1963, in the U.S. -- and the U.S. started giving shots. Mexico did not achieve measles elimination status until 2016.

Cases began surging in Mexico early last year. Researchers think an outbreak in the northern border state of Chihuahua stemmed from an unvaccinated Mennonite child from that Mexican state who traveled to Gaines County, Texas, where an outbreak was occurring, and came back infected.

As of this week, more than 20,500 confirmed cases have been reported across the nation. Fifty measles-related deaths have been reported, most of them children younger than 5 years old.

The issue has not been confined to North America. Early this year, the World Health Organization confirmed that Armenia, Austria, Azerbaijan, Spain, the United Kingdom, and Uzbekistan lost their status.
